- SEARCH LIMITATION: the shared web-search budget ran out partway through. Not checked: Zeus Apple-silicon support, Jetson per-layer energy papers, and classic CNN layer-energy work such as NeuralPower. Prior art in those areas is possible but was not found.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

Below, P1-P3 are mild, P4-P7 are serious, P8 and P9 are cheap fixes, and P10 is the verdict.

P1. Whole-layer arms are feasible, and the claim's numbers hold up. Per-token decode costs about 0.62 J on Qwen3-30B-A3B (ALIGN pilot 4). One of 36-48 layers is about 13-17 mJ per token, or about 13-17 J per 1,000 teacher-forced tokens. Replays repeat to about 2 J per measurement, with a pair-difference SD of about 3 J (pilot 5). So one layer skip is about 5 SD per pair. With about 10 interleaved pairs the SE is about 1 J, which pins a layer's marginal energy to roughly 5-8% of itself, or about 0.1 percentage points of total energy. Episodes of at least 1k tokens are needed; shorter ones fall under the floor. The '50 J member floor' quoted in the idea's detectability field does not appear in RUN_STATE.md or the question bank (only 50 J per doubling, 1638), so it is unsourced. The real anchor is the 2-3 J pair difference.

P2. Shares measured per module type across all layers are easy. Arms that skip all attention, skip all MLP, skip lm_head, or change context length move 10-60% of the bytes. For Qwen3-30B-A3B the published config (recalled from memory, not re-opened) gives per layer about 19M attention parameters, about 38M active-expert parameters and 0.26M router parameters. lm_head is about 311M, roughly 10% of active bytes. Recommend these all-layer module arms plus a few single-layer arms, rather than a full grid of layer by module.

P3. The router arm is ill-posed and cannot be detected. It is about 0.4% of active bytes in total, and its cost is mostly kernel launch and top-k overhead. Skipping it has no defined meaning, because experts still have to be chosen. Drop it, or replace it with replay of the base run's recorded expert indices.

P4. Weight edits break comparability in MoE models. Skipping layer L changes the hidden state, so later layers pick different experts, even under teacher forcing. That changes which expert weights stream and how often the system-level cache (SLC) hits, so the 'skip' contrast then includes a routing change. Fix: force routing to the base run's recorded expert indices in every arm. This is the same issue that makes MoE the only place where 'stable across prompt sets' is a real test. For a dense model with tokens and context fixed, weight streaming does not depend on the data except for switching power that varies with values (likely a few percent of the layer, and unmeasured). Prompt stability is then close to trivially true, so stating it as a finding would oversell it.

P5. 'GPU rail = compute, DC/rest = traffic' is the wrong mapping. Batch-1 decode is memory-bound. KB attempt 3 already shows GPU-rail energy scaling with bytes (0.00879 ± 0.00010 J/GB), so the GPU rail carries traffic cost as well. For decode GEMV, FLOPs are proportional to bytes (about 2 FLOP per parameter at a fixed quantization). That makes J/FLOP and J/GB collinear across module arms, so they cannot be separated. Separating them needs an axis that changes arithmetic intensity: batch size (more than 1), quantization width (4-bit vs 8-bit of the same module), or a prefill arm. Without one, report J/GB per byte stream only.

P6. The precision of the traffic share is overstated. The DC and rest classes in the KB run resolved at 0.0389 ± 0.0072 and 0.0296 ± 0.0084 J/GB, a relative uncertainty of about 18-28%. Only the GPU rail reached about 1%. So 'a few percent' holds for GPU-rail shares at layer or module-type level, and not for whole-machine traffic shares. Those come out at about 20-30% unless the episodes are made much longer. The 0.0001 J/GB anchor is a standard error on a coefficient, not an effect size.

P7. Removal is not additive. A skip arm finishes about 1/L sooner, so baseline and static power multiplied by the time saved gets credited to the removed module. That is minor on the GPU rail. On the DC meter, where the machine's baseline is several watts, it is large and could exceed the layer's dynamic energy. Changes in DVFS and duty cycle, and the CPU-side cost of encoding one fewer Metal kernel, also count. Fix: report marginal energy, gross and net of a measured idle baseline. Include a sum check (the sum of single-module marginals against the all-skip arm and the total) and call any gap 'non-additive'. Do not call the results 'shares'.

P8. 'Attention share grows with context' depends on the architecture. It holds for full-attention Qwen3 models. In Qwen3-Next and Flash-Next, about 3/4 of layers are linear attention (Gated DeltaNet, recalled from memory, not re-opened) with a fixed-size state, so only about 1/4 of attention grows with context. Write a separate prediction for each model. For full attention the result can be predicted from bytes alone: KV is about 2 KB per token of context per layer for 30B-A3B, about 0.8 GB per decoded token at 8K context across 48 layers. The information the experiment adds is whether J/GB for KV reads equals J/GB for weight streaming. SLC and access-pattern differences could make them differ, and that is the interesting part.

P9. Engineering is feasible. Identity bypass and forced routing go through the existing MLX model_file hook. Teacher forcing on recorded tokens is already used in the ALIGN replays. 0.5 Mac-night per model is plausible: 3 contexts × about 8 arms × about 10 repeats × about 60 s, plus cooldowns. The powermetrics cadence (about 100-150 ms) is fine for episodes of 1k tokens or more, and too coarse for any per-kernel attribution. That is why only removal arms work, not in-step timing.

P10. Verdict on the noise worry Ed raised. At fixed tokens, surgical value edits like Astra's cannot be resolved on any of our channels, because almost nothing physical changes. Structural edits and the behavioural changes that follow from edits (output length, routing) are well above the floor. So the study should send interpretability edits through behaviour counts times the JOULE-MAP price list, and not try to detect them directly in power. The idea is feasible after these changes: per-module-type arms, forced routing, an axis that changes arithmetic intensity, marginal energy rather than shares, a stated whole-machine uncertainty of about 20-30%, and context predictions written separately for each architecture.

**Residual novelty**

I found no prior work that measures marginal energy per layer and per module by removing them inside a live decode (identity bypass, teacher-forced, with the arms interleaved). I also found none on Apple unified memory with rails and a DC meter, and none that gives J/GB per module set against bytes moved. Closest work: HYMELL (NVML, each kernel run in isolation, H100), analytical estimators (Tokens to Watt-hours), whole-model Apple powermetrics benchmarks, and system-level diagnosis (Where Do the Joules Go). The idea is not refuted. Most of the novelty is in the method and the platform, not in the qualitative findings. 'MLP dominates at short context' and 'attention share grows with context' already follow from byte accounting and from GPU latency studies, and our own ALIGN attention-length fit already contains the context term. The parts worth keeping: (a) an energy price list per module on unified memory, in J/GB per byte stream (weights, KV, lm_head) on the GPU rail and on the whole machine; (b) a test of whether in-situ removal adds up, i.e. whether the sum of the marginal energies equals the total; (c) for Ed's interest in interpretability, a baseline that predicts a weight edit's energy effect from its structure. That last part is the most interesting new angle. Astra-style surgical edits change values, not shapes, so at fixed tokens they should change per-token energy by about zero, below every floor we have. Their whole energy effect comes through behaviour: output length (Astra's edit cut cap-hitting from 93% to 33%) and, in MoE models, routing. The useful study is therefore count-plus-price: JOULE-MAP supplies the price per module and per token, and the ALIGN counts-logged fit supplies the counts. Structural edits (layer, head or expert pruning) move bytes and fall in the range where JOULE-MAP itself can detect them. That frames power as a signal we can predict from structure and behaviour, rather than one we have to dig out of machine noise.

---

## 5. PRECISION-TAMPER: energy fingerprint of covert precision or sparsity substitution (seat 1 (Fable))

### Fable 5.1 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- OPENED. Cai, Shi, Zhao, Song, 'Are You Getting What You Pay For? Auditing Model Substitution in LLM APIs', arXiv:2504.04715 (v2 Sep 2025). Names quantized versions explicitly as the covert substitute; finds output-statistics tests query-intensive and weak against subtle substitutions, log-prob tests defeated by nondeterminism; recommends TEEs. Energy/power is NOT considered as an audit channel. This is the closest framing match and leaves the energy channel open.
- OPENED. Husom et al., 'Sustainable LLM Inference for Edge AI: Evaluating Quantized LLMs for Energy Efficiency, Output Accuracy, and Inference Latency', arXiv:2504.03360 (Apr 2025). Raspberry Pi 4 + Joulescope JS110, Ollama/llama.cpp. LLaMA-3.2-1B: FP16 17.60 J/tok, Q8_0 7.87, Q4_K_M 7.11, Q3_K_S 3.75 (Q4 vs Q8 only ~10%); Qwen2.5-0.5B: Q8_0 2.14 J/tok vs Q4_K_M 2.56 (Q4 WORSE than Q8, ~16%). Huge SDs (±5.36 J/tok). Substantially answers the magnitude half of the claim ('precision changes J/token') and shows the 8->4 step is NOT reliably >5% nor even monotone on CPU paths; does not touch detection or Apple silicon.
- OPENED (abstract only). Husom, Goknil, Shar, Sen, 'The Price of Prompting: Profiling Energy Use in LLM Inference', arXiv:2407.16893 (MELODI). Energy-vs-prompt profiling; no quantization numbers on the abstract page. Already cited inside JouleWise (docs/run_reports/2026-07-30-sweep-mechanisms.md:47) together with 2504.03360 as '4-bit gains 1.35-2.95x total energy vs unquantized' and the 'q4 worse than q8 dequant-bottleneck anomaly worth testing'.
- OPENED (abstract only). Samsi et al., 'From Words to Watts', arXiv:2310.03003. V100/A100 LLaMA energy benchmarking; no precision contrasts. General background only.
- OPENED. Egashira, Vero, Staab, He, Vechev, 'Exploiting LLM Quantization', arXiv:2405.18137 (NeurIPS 2024). Weights crafted so the full-precision model is benign and the quantized one is malicious. Relevant as the threat model that makes 'which precision is actually running' a safety question; does not address detection via any side channel.
- OPENED. Batina, Bhasin, Jap, Picek, 'CSI NN', USENIX Security 2019: EM + timing side channel recovers layer count, neuron counts, activations and weights of MLPs/CNNs on Cortex-M3. OPENED. Duddu et al., 'Stealing Neural Networks via Timing Side Channels', arXiv:1812.11720: timing recovers depth/architecture. Neither addresses LLM precision/quantization; they establish the class 'physical side channels recover model structure', so an energy-vs-precision fingerprint is an incremental instance, not a new class. DeepSniffer (Hu et al., ASPLOS 2020, GPU bus/power side channel -> architecture) UNVERIFIED: ACM page returned 403.
- OPENED. Takehi, Teoh, Zhang, Khattab (MIT CSAIL), 'Sparks of Weight-Space Programming: LLMs can natively read & write neural network weights', https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/ (2 Nov 2026); logs at github.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs. This IS Ed's 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights' reference (secondary coverage: it-connect.tech, shiftdelete.net, towardsai). Astra edited 131,072 of ~0.8B weights (0.016%) of Qwen3.5-0.8B in MLP layers 15 and 19 (subtracted one direction from 64 output columns each), no training: MATH-500 7.9%->48.1%, GSM8K 13.8%->67.1%, IFEval 21.0%->62.3%; 32,768-token truncation rate 93.0%->33.1%. On Gemma-3-270M MATH-500 only 6.8%->7.7%. No energy, power, speed or dtype statements anywhere in the report. Note the edit's dominant effect is on OUTPUT LENGTH (looping), i.e. energy per TASK collapses while J/token at fixed tokens should be unchanged: exactly the dissociation the idea predicts, and a free natural experiment the design should exploit.
- OPENED (JouleWise internal). docs/research_question_bank.md: C5-1.12 'Quantization benefit decomposition, Mac leg' (4/8-bit MLX, power vs time split, n>=5), RQ-D-A07 'Weight compression x state compression' (Qwen3-32B 4/8-bit weights x 4/8-bit KV, >12 J interaction gate), C5-2.11/2.14 KV-quant riders, PC-9 'quantization split (watts vs time)'; PC-4: 'decode is bandwidth-bound at near-flat power (~23-28 W from 1.5B to 122B-A10B), so decode energy differences are decode time'. The magnitude measurement is already a banked, un-run JouleWise question; PRECISION-TAMPER re-skins it as an audit.
- OPENED (JouleWise internal). docs/process_traces/2026-10-31-magistrate-5cbfbcfc/KB-SLOT-FILL.md: GPU rail 0.00879 +/- 0.00010 J/GB, DC 0.0389 +/- 0.0072 J/GB, measured with 32 MiB latency-bound dispatches at 106-116 GB/s; 85% of the GPU contrast is stall time (~10 W for 25 us longer), and the record states 'transfer to bandwidth-bound decode traffic is a stated limit of this test'. docs/process_traces/2026-11-03-magistrate-1b198a71/RECORD.md and 2026-11-04-magistrate-7e06863b/RECORD.md: ALIGN five-term fit e = 0.618 +/- 0.013 J/decode token, s_r 177 J, same-position identical-work noise 2.0 J (1.4 J unflagged), sigma_w ~5 J across K=3 windows, reproducible session-position replay excess +1.3 to +10 J; token identity across windows is by deterministic regeneration checked with first_divergence, NOT by forced decoding.
- NOT FOUND (search budget exhausted after 3 queries; stated plainly): no paper found that uses energy/power telemetry to detect quantization, pruning or expert-count substitution in a deployed LLM, on any hardware. A paper 'Pre-Registering the Detectable Effect: A Paired-MDE Budget for 4-bit Quantization Benchmarks' (arXiv:2605.28873) is cited in docs/paper/draft-v1.md ref 26 as accuracy-only; UNVERIFIED here.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1. SIGNAL SIZE IS NOT THE QUESTION. On this instrument the 8->4-bit contrast is overwhelming: PC-4 says decode runs at near-flat ~23-28 W and is bandwidth-bound, so dE/token ~ P x d(bytes)/BW. For the v4 kit model (mlx-community/Qwen3.8-27B-8bit, ~27 GB/token) going to 4-bit saves ~13.5 GB/token; at ~400 GB/s that is ~34 ms x 25 W ~ 0.8 J/token, i.e. hundreds of J on a 1,000-token turn against a 2 J same-position noise floor and 5 J sigma_w. The KB route gives a smaller number (13.5 GB x 0.0389 J/GB ~ 0.5 J/token DC, 0.12 J/token GPU rail) but the KB coefficient was measured latency-bound at ~110 GB/s and its own record says it does not transfer to bandwidth-bound decode; do not cite KB as the predicted size, cite PC-4 time-at-flat-power, and treat agreement between the two as a side finding. Either way detection in a single replay is at >50 sigma: the 'detectable' half is a demonstration, not a hypothesis, and the 5% threshold is unmotivated (the real question is whether the five-term residual, fit on benign data, reads a precision swap as a NEGATIVE tamper score at all, since its bytes term and e coefficient would silently refit if the swap is in the training set). 2. 'FIXED TOKENS' IS NOT CURRENTLY ACHIEVABLE WITH A SUBSTITUTED MODEL. ALIGN gets token identity by deterministic regeneration of the same seeds and checks it with first_divergence; a 4-bit, pruned or expert-collapsed model will diverge within a few tokens, so J/token at fixed tokens requires teacher-forced decoding of the declared trajectory's output ids (the #657 prefill_prompt hook is the nearest existing path). Without that, the arm compares different token streams and the residual's length/term structure absorbs the effect. This is a kit change, not a 0.3-night registration reuse. 3. THE SURGICAL-EDIT NULL IS NEAR-TAUTOLOGICAL AT FIXED TOKENS. An in-place same-dtype edit of 0.016% of weights changes no bytes, shapes or FLOPs; the only residual physics is data-dependent switching power (operand bit patterns in the GPU datapath and on the memory bus), which for 0.016% of weights is far below 0.1%. Registering it as a prediction buys nothing. The non-trivial version is PRUNING STORED DENSE: zeros change neither bytes nor FLOPs (MLX has no unstructured-sparse skip path that I know of; UNVERIFIED), so the idea's premise 'pruned => fewer bytes => >5%' is false unless tensors are physically shrunk or sparse kernels exist; conversely 50% zero operands could lower dynamic power by a few percent via reduced toggling (GPU data-dependent power is documented in general, UNVERIFIED for Apple GPUs), and at a 0.1% floor that IS resolvable. That would falsify 'energy audits precision and work, not weights' and is the one genuinely open physics question here. Also check Husom's non-monotonicity: on CPU paths Q4 was 16% WORSE than Q8 for Qwen2.5-0.5B; on MLX the 4-bit affine kernels are usually faster, but for small models the dequant compute term can flatten the contrast, so predict per-model, not globally. 4. CONFOUNDS A DISHONEST PROVIDER CONTROLS: KV-cache precision (kv_bits) halves KV bytes without touching weights; thermal state (long episodes dropped decode clock 1380->600 MHz in pilot records, i.e. tens of percent on time at roughly constant energy per byte only if power scales too); batch/concurrency; a 4-bit model file also changes page-cache/mmap behaviour on load, so the contrast must be steady-state decode only. Expert-collapse depends on router top-k, so 'expert-collapsed' must be defined as a fixed top-k reduction, not a trained collapse. 5. THREAT-MODEL FEASIBILITY: powermetrics and a DC meter require root on, or physical access to, the serving machine; anyone with that can hash the weight files, which is cheaper and exact. The energy audit is only worth something when (a) weights are swapped in memory after load-time attestation, (b) the serving box is a sealed appliance or enclave exposing only a power rail, or (c) it is used as a specificity/positive control for ALIGN: the five-term residual must flag precision substitution as a negative-energy anomaly distinct from stripped-token tampering. Frame (c) is the one that fits the existing machinery; (a)/(b) belong in a threat-model paragraph, not the claim. 6. Ed's noise worry is correct in general and wrong for this effect: the 2 J floor is achieved only under the ALIGN quiet regime (Spotlight SIGSTOP, census, exclusions); the Spotlight burst in pilot 5 moved single members by +9.6 J and CPU by +56 J, which is still 10-50x below the precision contrast. The surgical-edit null and the dense-pruning question are the only parts where machine noise is a limiting factor.

**Residual novelty**

Not refuted, but the headline claim should be re-cut. What is already known: precision changes J/token by tens of percent (Husom 2025 on Pi; JouleWise's own banked C5-1.12/RQ-D-A07/PC-9 on Mac), and model substitution including quantization is an open audit problem whose text-based detectors fail (Cai et al. 2025, who propose TEEs and never consider energy). What no opened source does: use energy telemetry as the substitution detector, or test the dissociation 'bytes/precision moves energy, surgical weight edits do not'. Residual novelty worth keeping, in order: (1) ALIGN specificity control: show the registered five-term residual reads an 8-bit->4-bit swap of the v4 kit model as a large negative anomaly at fixed (teacher-forced) tokens, distinct from stripped-token tampering, with the KB bytes term vs PC-4 time-at-flat-power compared as two predictions of the same contrast (the KB record itself flags the bandwidth-bound transfer as untested). (2) The dense-pruning arm as the real physics question: identical bytes and FLOPs, 30-50% zero weights, is data-dependent switching power resolvable at the 2 J floor? A non-null there falsifies 'energy audits work, not weights'. (3) The Astra natural experiment: Takehi et al.'s 0.016% edit mainly changes output LENGTH (93% -> 33% hitting the 32k cap), so energy per TASK collapses while J/token at fixed tokens should be unchanged; replicating that dissociation on an MLX port of Qwen3.5-0.8B (edit vector from the public logs) is cheap, directly addresses Ed's interp intersection, and is the only arm where the 'surgical edit' is a real published edit rather than a synthetic one. Drop: the 5% threshold, the 'single replay' framing (trivially true), and the KB coefficient as the size predictor. Cost is more than 0.3 night once teacher-forced replay is added to the kit; bank as a v5 ALIGN rider and record C5-1.12/RQ-D-A07 as the lineage in docs/research_question_bank.md.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Cai, Shi, Zhao, Song, 'Are You Getting What You Pay For? Auditing Model Substitution in LLM APIs', arXiv:2504.04715 (Apr 2025, rev. Sep 2025). Opened (abstract). Treats quantized and smaller models as substitutes. Finds output-based and logprob tests unreliable and recommends TEEs. The abstract mentions no energy or timing channel. It is the problem framing, not an energy method.
- Gao, Liang, Guestrin, 'Model Equality Testing: Which Model Is This API Serving?', arXiv:2410.20247 (Oct 2024, rev. Apr 2025). Opened (abstract). An MMD two-sample test on outputs detects distortions such as quantization at about 10 samples per prompt, and 11 of 31 commercial endpoints differed from the reference weights. It is an output-channel competitor to an energy audit.
- Ong et al., 'TOPLOC: A Locality Sensitive Hashing Scheme for Trustless Verifiable Inference', arXiv:2501.16007 (Jan 2025). Opened (abstract). Hashing activations detects unauthorized changes to model, prompt or precision with 100% accuracy in their evaluations. Where activations are available, it already answers 'was precision covertly changed', and more strongly than energy can.
- Xiang et al., 'Open DNN Box by Power Side-Channel Attack', arXiv:1907.10406 (2019). Opened (abstract). Power side channels on embedded devices recover a DNN's architecture and estimate its parameters (about 96.5%). This is precedent for 'energy reveals architecture and work'.
- Gregersen, Patel, Choukse, 'Input-Dependent Power Usage in GPUs', arXiv:2409.18324 (Sep 2024). Opened (abstract). At fixed shapes, GEMM power varies by almost 40% with value distribution, bit similarity, placement and sparsity, attributed to bit flips (H100-class GPUs). This contradicts a blanket 'energy does not see weights', and it bears directly on the pruning arm.
- Taneja et al., 'Hot Pixels: Frequency, Power, and Temperature Attacks on GPUs and ARM SoCs', arXiv:2305.12784 (2023). Opened (abstract). On-chip power, temperature and frequency sensors on SoCs correlate with the instructions executed and the data processed. The abstract names no Apple chips, so the Apple coverage is UNVERIFIED.
- Ed's reference was found. Takehi, Teoh, Zhang, Khattab (MIT CSAIL), 'Sparks of Weight-Space Programming' blog, rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/ (Nov 2026). Opened. GPT-6 Astra edited Qwen3.5-0.8B with no gradient descent: 131,072 weights (0.016%), removing one MLP-written direction from 64 output columns in each of layers 15 and 19. This cured looping, and MATH-500 rose from 7.9% to 48.1%. The post states no dtype and reports no energy or latency measurements. Secondary coverage opened: shiftdelete.net/en/gpt-6-astra-weights. Not opened (HTTP 403): yourstory.com and buildmvpfast.com. The claim that OpenAI halted a 'GPT-6.1 Astra' appears only in search snippets and is UNVERIFIED.
- LIMITATION: the session's web-search budget ran out after the first query. The papers above were reached by direct arXiv fetches of IDs recalled from memory, and each was opened and confirmed. I could not search specifically for prior energy- or power-meter-based detection of LLM substitution or quantization, so the absence of such work is not established.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1) The requant arm (8-bit to 4-bit) passes the threshold easily, but energy is not needed to see it. Worked example on the ALIGN model (Qwen3-30B-A3B, about 3.3B active parameters; MLX affine q4 at group 64 is about 4.5 bits per weight, q8 about 8.5): active weight bytes are about 1.86 GB/token at 4-bit and about 3.5 GB/token at 8-bit, a difference of about 1.65 GB/token. Decode is memory-bound, so tokens per second drop by roughly 1.5 to 2 times and J/token rises by tens of percent on GPU and DC. Over a 2-4 kJ episode that is hundreds of joules, against a 2 J replay floor and about 1% ALIGN residual. Expect about 100 times the noise in a single replay, even with background load. The catch is wall-clock time per token, which shows the same substitution with no meter. The energy channel adds information only if the substitute pads latency to match the declared model's timing. That timing-masked arm (a 4-bit model padded with idle or spin to 8-bit latency) is the one that tests the claim, and the current design does not include it.
2) The KB coefficients are misused as the size predictor. KB attempt 3 measured 'rotate minus repeat' J/GB: the marginal energy of DRAM traffic against cache-resident traffic in a latency-bound probe, recorded in docs/process_traces/2026-10-31-magistrate-5cbfbcfc/RECORD.md and KB-SLOT-FILL.md. Applied to the 1.65 GB/token difference, it predicts only about 0.0145 J/token on the GPU rail. Pilot-3 five-term decode is 0.158-0.279 J/token (af17111e RECORD), so that is roughly 5-9%. On DC it predicts about 0.064 J/token. The 'tens of percent' expectation comes instead from time multiplied by static and uncore power, plus the cost of the dequant kernels. The proposal's two size claims contradict each other. Register both, and treat the gap between the fitted bytes-term slope and the KB coefficient as the estimand. A large gap would be a real finding: the KB coefficient does not price decode bytes.
3) The pruning arm is mis-specified. Unstructured or magnitude pruning in MLX keeps the dense tensors, so bytes and work per token are unchanged. The bytes term predicts about 0 and the arm cannot reach 5% by that mechanism. Data-dependent power (Gregersen et al., up to about 40% in GEMMs on H100) could produce a signal from bit-flip and zero density, but its direction and size on Apple GPU memory-bound decode are unknown. Structured pruning (fewer layers or narrower widths) reduces bytes and would be detected. Pre-register which kind each arm uses, with separate predictions.
4) The expert-collapse arm is also mis-specified. Merging or dropping experts while keeping top-k leaves the active bytes per token nearly unchanged, so the predicted effect is null. The system-level cache is tens of MB against GB-scale experts, so locality gains are negligible. Only a reduction in top-k (for example 8 to 4) halves the expert bytes and is detectable. 'Expert-collapsed' has to be defined as a top-k change, or it should be expected to be a null.
5) 'Fixed tokens' needs forced decoding. A substituted model emits different tokens and different lengths, and quantized models may loop more (exactly the behaviour Astra fixed). Free generation confounds the comparison with token count. Arms must teacher-force an identical transcript, or use fixed-length generation with ignore-EOS and identical prompts. Otherwise J/token differences mix per-token cost with the content-dependent attention and KV cost.
6) 'Surgical edit is null' is nearly true by construction at fixed tokens: same shapes, kernels and bytes, and an edit of about 0.016% of weights barely changes bit-flip statistics. It is therefore a weak test. Where Astra-style edits do matter for energy is per task, not per token: removing looping cuts tokens and so cuts J per task, possibly by large factors. Saying energy audits 'not weights' is accurate only per token.
7) Threat-model gap. On a local Mac whose rails you can meter, you can also hash the weight file, so a covert local substitution is not a realistic adversary. On a remote API you cannot meter the provider's rails. The realistic use is the reverse direction: checking a provider's declared J/token or energy disclosures against the declared model, precision and active bytes. The design should be framed that way.
8) Cost of about 0.3 Mac-night is plausible: four or five arms, a few forced-decode replays each, and an 8-bit 30B-A3B at about 32 GB fits in 128 GB. The prereq to watch is disk and offload of the extra quantized variants, which the 11-07 SSD covers.

**Residual novelty**

Partial novelty, not refuted. The adjacent ground is already covered. Output-based substitution audits exist (Gao et al. 2024; Cai et al. 2025), and activation-hash verification already catches precision changes (TOPLOC 2025). Power side channels revealing architecture (Xiang et al. 2019) and data-dependent GPU power (Gregersen et al. 2024) are published. That quantization lowers J/token is well known, and timing alone exposes a plain 8-to-4-bit swap. Within the papers I could open (the search budget was exhausted, so this is unconfirmed), nobody has measured the following on Apple silicon with metered rails and DC input, under replay-grade noise control:
(a) a calibrated, pre-registered mapping from declared active bytes per token, through quantization, top-k and structured pruning, to J/token, including the gap between the fitted bytes slope and the KB 'rotate minus repeat' coefficient;
(b) a timing-masked substitution arm (a padded 4-bit model passing as 8-bit), which is the only case where energy beats the stopwatch;
(c) data-dependent power on Apple GPU decode, from unstructured pruning and sparsity at fixed bytes, where the size and even the direction are unknown;
(d) the split that connects to Ed's interpretability interest: Astra-style surgical edits are predicted null in J/token, while J per task can change a lot through behaviour (fewer looping tokens).
Recommended reshape: rename the arms (top-k reduction rather than 'expert collapse'; unstructured and structured pruning registered separately), teacher-force a fixed transcript, add the timing-masked arm, and frame the use case as checking declared energy against the declared model rather than catching covert local tampering.

---

## 6. ROUTER-EDIT: MoE routing edits as an energy channel (seat 1 (Fable))

### Fable 5.1 checker: REFUTED

**Prior work**

- JouleWise RQ bank (opened: /Users/edr/code/CapstoneProject/jw-wt/handoff/docs/research_question_bank.md ~L1720-1726, L1795): RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH already poses this exact mechanism (routing concentration -> expert weight reuse) on Qwen3-30B-A3B with a PREDECLARED NULL at B=1 ('expert >> system-level cache, no reuse'); the D-A03 merge row adopts that B=1 null as the sharper falsifier. RQ-D-A02 (L1263-1281) covers function-preserving expert dispatch-layout/locality effects; RQ-D-A03 (L1283-1301) covers the expert-union batching penalty; RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB (L1632-1640) covers top-k as the active-bytes dial and names the same MLX router-hook prerequisite. ROUTER-EDIT is a subset of these.
- mlx-lm source (opened: raw.githubusercontent.com/ml-explore/mlx-lm/main/mlx_lm/models/switch_layers.py and qwen3_moe.py): router = nn.Linear + softmax, top-k via mx.argpartition (k distinct indices), norm_topk_prob; experts computed with mx.gather_qmm over a stacked [E,out,in] tensor; tokens are sorted by expert only when indices.size >= 64 (never at B=1 decode); no shared expert. Confirms per-token expert bytes are fixed by k, independent of which experts are selected.
- Qwen3-30B-A3B config.json (opened: huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B/raw/main/config.json): 48 layers, 128 experts, top-8, hidden 2048, moe_intermediate 768, no shared expert -> ~4.7M params/expert (~2.65 MB at 4-bit), ~21 MB expert bytes/layer/token, ~1.0 GB expert bytes/token, ~1.6 GB total bytes/token, ~16-17 GB resident.
- Huang et al. 2023, 'Towards MoE Deployment: Mitigating Inefficiencies in MoE Inference', arXiv:2303.06182 (opened): expert buffering keeps 'hot' experts in GPU memory, 1.47x static memory reduction, 6-11x throughput via dynamic gating; latency/memory only, no energy. The routing-locality intuition in ROUTER-EDIT comes from this offloading/serving regime, which does not apply to a fully resident unified-memory Mac.
- Yi et al. 2023/2025, 'EdgeMoE: Empowering Sparse LLMs on Mobile Devices', arXiv:2308.14352 (opened): expert weights on external storage fetched on activation, expert preloading; memory/speedup, energy not stated in abstract. Again a paging regime, not the resident case.
- Lu et al. 2024 (ACL), 'Not All Experts are Equal: Efficient Expert Pruning and Skipping for MoE LLMs', arXiv:2402.14800 (opened): post-training expert pruning/skipping reduces size and increases speed; no energy measurement.
- Lasby et al. 2025, 'REAP the Experts', arXiv:2510.13999 (opened): router-weighted expert activation pruning, 50% expert removal near-lossless on Qwen3-Coder-480B and Kimi-K2; accuracy only, no energy/speed; abstract does not name Qwen3-30B-A3B.
- Fayyaz et al. 2025 (ICLR 2026), 'Steering MoE LLMs via Expert (De)Activation' (SteerMoE), arXiv:2509.09660 (opened): test-time expert (de)activation shifts safety/faithfulness across 6 MoE LLMs and 11 benchmarks; this is the 'surgical routing edit changes performance' half of Ed's intersection, with no energy measurement.
- Hayes, Shumailov, Yona 2024, 'Buffer Overflow in Mixture of Experts', arXiv:2402.05526 (opened): cross-batch routing dependencies let malicious queries alter benign outputs; no energy.
- Shumailov et al. 2020/2021, 'Sponge Examples: Energy-Latency Attacks on Neural Networks', arXiv:2006.03463 (opened): adversarial inputs raise energy 10-200x on CPU/GPU/ASIC-sim; not MoE-specific; the only published line that measures data-dependent energy directly.
- Muennighoff et al. 2024, 'OLMoE', arXiv:2409.02060 (opened abstract only): routing analysis showing high expert specialization; no energy.
- Apple M4 family memory bandwidth (opened: en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_M4): M4 120 GB/s, M4 Pro 273 GB/s, M4 Max 546 GB/s (410 binned); SLC size NOT stated there. Apple SLC = tens of MB is my recollection: UNVERIFIED.
- UNVERIFIED (not opened, web search budget exhausted this session): MoE-Infinity arXiv:2401.14361 (expert activation traces for offloading), ExpertFlow arXiv:2410.17954, Pre-gated MoE arXiv:2308.12066, any 'MoE-ERAS'-style expert residency scheduling work.
- Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': could NOT be verified. WebSearch budget was exhausted before the query ran and openai.com/index/gpt-6/ returned HTTP 403. Treat as unconfirmed; do not cite it in research docs without a source.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

The claim as stated (B=1 teacher-forced decode, fixed top-k, fully resident Qwen3-30B-A3B on unified memory) has no physical channel. (1) Bytes per token are fixed by k: argpartition returns 8 distinct expert ids per layer, so every token gathers exactly 8 x ~2.65 MB = ~21 MB of expert weights per layer (~1.0 GB expert + ~0.6 GB attention/embedding = ~1.6 GB/token) regardless of WHICH experts; routing entropy from collapse to uniform cannot change the byte count. (2) Cross-token reuse is impossible: for layer L's expert selection to still be on-chip when the next token reaches layer L, it must survive ~1.6 GB of intervening traffic; Apple SLC is tens of MB (UNVERIFIED figure, but certainly << 1.6 GB), so expert weights stream from DRAM every token in every arm. This is exactly the repo's own predeclared B=1 null (RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH). (3) 'Residency' is moot: 16-17 GB resident fits a 128 GB Mac with no paging; the expert-buffering/offload literature (Huang 2023, EdgeMoE) from which the intuition is borrowed only applies when experts are not all resident. (4) The detectability estimate is therefore wrong by construction: 'GB-scale traffic differences per 1k tokens' are zero at B=1; per 1k tokens all arms move ~1.6 TB identically. Second-order residuals that could survive: DRAM row/page locality for adjacent vs scattered expert ids (each expert is a 2.65 MB contiguous stream, so row-buffer behaviour is near-identical; expected << 1%), and data-dependent bit-toggle power from changed hidden-state values (typically sub-percent on GPUs; no zero-skipping in MLX quantized matmul). With replays repeating to ~2 J on 2-4 kJ episodes (~0.05-0.1%) a sub-percent effect is borderline measurable but would need ~10+ paired replays per arm and would not be attributable to 'routing diversity' without a dense-model toggle-power control. Where the mechanism IS real: (a) B>1 decode: expected unique experts per layer for B=16 under uniform routing ~82 (217 MB/layer, ~10.4 GB/step) vs 8 under collapse (1.0 GB/step); at ~0.1 J/GB (repo scale: ~47 J for ~435 GB on the 1.5B leg) that is ~1 J/step, ~1 kJ per 1k steps, trivially detectable; but this is RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH / RQ-D-A03, already banked. (b) Prefill: 2048 tokens under uniform routing touch all 128 experts (~16 GB expert read) vs ~1 GB under collapse, plus gather_qmm sort-path (indices.size >= 64) group-size efficiency differences; ~1.5 J bytes-side per 2048-token prefill is at the 2 J repeat floor and compute-dominated, so marginal. (c) Memory-pressure Macs (e.g. 122B-A10B on a 36-64 GB machine) where routing concentration governs paging; large effect but OS-paging noise and not the stated design. Feasibility notes: the MLX router hook (override gates before argpartition) is the same desk prerequisite already listed for TOPK-KNOB and ROUTING×BATCH, so the 0.3 Mac-night cost is honest, but the B=1 arms would spend it confirming a predeclared null.

**Residual novelty**

Low as proposed; the energy half is already in the bank and predicted null at B=1. What is not yet in the bank and is worth filing in future-research docs: (1) Explicitly recast the B=1 null as a cheap predeclared falsifier rider on RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB (same hook, same window): 'at B=1 on resident unified memory, routing edits are energy-free; energy is set by k and bytes, not by which experts'. A confirmed null is itself a useful on-device result for the interp-edit story: SteerMoE-style expert (de)activation edits change behaviour at zero energy cost at B=1, while top-k edits change energy ~linearly with k. (2) The genuinely new intersection Ed cares about is weight-edit -> (performance, energy) jointly, and the energy-bearing edits are those that change BYTES, not routing: (a) top-k override (TOPK-KNOB, banked); (b) REAP-style expert pruning, which cuts resident bytes but not active bytes per token, a clean test of the BYTES-LAW 'resident bytes cost nothing' term plus a measured quality cost; (c) expert skipping with a per-token dynamic k (Lu et al. 2024 style), which turns routing confidence into an active-bytes dial and so is the one routing-side edit that CAN move B=1 energy; (d) data-dependent toggle power from steering edits, only as a sub-percent methodological question. (3) The B>1 and prefill arms (expert-union penalty, routing concentration under batching) should stay with RQ-D-A03 / RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH rather than be duplicated; ROUTER-EDIT should be filed as a note under those, not as a new experiment. (4) Record plainly that the 'GPT-6 Astra edits its own weights' premise is unverified in this session.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Skliar, van Rozendaal, Lepert, Boinovski, van Baalen, Nagel, Whatmough, Ehteshami Bejnordi, 'Mixture of Cache-Conditional Experts for Efficient Mobile Device Inference', arXiv 2412.00099 / TMLR June 2025 (abstract opened). Training-free routing that favours cached experts on memory-limited phones, so only part of the expert set fits in DRAM. It reports about 2x speedup. The abstract does not mention energy. This is the router-biasing intervention, but in the offload regime.
- Choi, Kim, Oh, Park, Kim, Yoo, 'SliceMoE: Bit-Sliced Expert Caching under Miss-Rate Constraints', arXiv 2512.12990 (HTML opened). Reports 2.37-2.85x lower decode energy on DeepSeek-V2-Lite and Qwen1.5-MoE. Experts that miss the cache are read from UFS flash (103 pJ/bit vs 1.5 pJ/bit for LPDDR4). The energy appears to come from a datasheet-based model, not from measurement. The paper describes Skliar et al.'s 'Cache-Prior' as boosting the gating scores of cached experts. The saving comes from avoiding flash reads, a channel that does not exist when the whole model is resident.
- Hoang, Jaiswal, Samragh, Cho, 'SpecMD: A Comprehensive Study On Speculative Expert Prefetching', arXiv 2602.03921 (abstract opened). Expert access does not follow LRU/LFU temporal locality. Experts are offloaded to a small VRAM cache. No energy is reported.
- Eliseev & Mazur, 'Fast Inference of Mixture-of-Experts Language Models with Offloading', arXiv 2312.17238 (abstract opened). Offloading on desktop/Colab hardware. The abstract does not confirm an LRU cache or expert reuse between adjacent tokens. No energy.
- NPUMoE, arXiv 2604.18788 (UNVERIFIED: seen only in a search snippet). Apple Neural Engine MoE prefill with reported energy-efficiency gains. It covers prefill, not decode locality.
- JouleWise's own bank (docs/research_question_bank.md) already contains this mechanism. RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH predicts a null at B=1 ('expert >> system-level cache, no reuse'). RQ-D-A02 covers function-preserving expert locality, RQ-D-A03 the expert-union batching penalty, and RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB uses top-k as a bytes dial. ROUTER-EDIT is mostly a restatement of the B=1 arm that the bank already expects to be null.
- Ed's reference, 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': NOT FOUND. The web-search budget for this session ran out before I could search for it. The repo mentions gpt-6-astra only as a consult model, not as weight-editing research. Treat the claim as unverified.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

At B=1 on a fully resident model, the proposed mechanism should produce essentially nothing. The detectability estimate is off by 2-3 orders of magnitude.

(1) Bytes per token do not depend on routing. Model config fetched from Hugging Face: hidden 2048, moe_intermediate 768, 128 experts, top-8, 48 layers, no shared expert. One expert has 3x2048x768 = 4.72M params. At 4-bit with group-64 scales, that is about 2.65 MB. Eight experts per layer is about 21 MB, so about 1.02 GB of expert weights per token (about 1.7 GB with attention and embeddings), whatever the routing entropy. The full expert pool is about 16 GB and fits in 128 GB of unified memory, so flash, misses and offload never happen. Every prior-work energy saving comes from that offload channel.

(2) No cache reuse is possible. The BYTES known-bytes test on the M3 Max found the GPU-visible cache-resident rung at 32 MiB, with 64 MiB unresolved. Even under total collapse (the same 8 experts in every layer), the reuse distance for any expert is one full forward pass of about 1.7 GB. That is about 30-50x the cache. With cyclic or LRU-like access, about 0 hits; the best possible policy gets at most about 3-6%.

(3) The size of the effect is bounded by the measured anchors. Rotate minus repeat (DRAM vs cache hit) cost 0.0088 J/GB on the GPU rail and 0.039 J/GB at DC (KB-SLOT-FILL, magistrate 5cbfbcfc). The impossible case of 100% hits gives at most 9 mJ/token (GPU) or 40 mJ/token (DC). A realistic ceiling of 6% hits or less gives at most 0.5 mJ/token (GPU) or 2.4 mJ/token (DC). Both are below the bank's 2.93 mJ/token bar (6 J per 2048-token window), and the expected value is about 0.

The idea's 'GB-scale per 1k tokens -> tens of J' assumes about 10+ J/GB. That is about 1000x the measured marginal 0.009-0.039 J/GB and about 100x the all-in decode cost of about 0.1 J/GB (planning level: 47 J per 512 tokens on 1.5B at 4-bit, about 0.85 GB/token). Also, 1 GB out of about 1.7 TB of traffic per 1k tokens is 0.06%. A '50 J member' is only a few hundred tokens, so the best-case signal there is about 0.1 J against about 2 J of replay noise. It cannot be detected.

(4) The MLX dispatch does not change. In mlx-lm SwitchGLU, do_sort = indices.size >= 64. At B=1 decode indices.size = 8, so the gather_qmm launches and shapes are the same for any routing.

Design trap: if 'teacher-forced decode' runs as one parallel forward over the sequence, indices.size exceeds 64, the sorted path engages, and the union of experts per layer (8 up to 128, i.e. about 1 GB vs 16 GB per pass) does change bytes. That is a prefill/batch effect, already covered by RQ-D-A03 and ROUTING×BATCH, not a decode-locality effect. Teacher forcing must be done token by token with a KV cache.

(5) Confounds: perturbing the router changes activation statistics, so data-dependent switching power could produce a small non-null that has nothing to do with locality. Collapse or bias edits also change outputs. In free generation, any change in output length would swamp per-token physics. A wired-memory or compression state left uncontrolled could fake a difference between collapsed and uniform runs.

Feasible version: about 0.3 Mac-night as a pre-declared null, i.e. an upper bound. It is the B=1 arm of RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH, with expert IDs logged and token-by-token teacher forcing, and it needs long decodes (thousands of tokens per member) to bound below about 0.5 mJ/token.

**Residual novelty**

Nothing I opened gives a measured bound on how much routing locality changes decode energy when all experts are resident in unified memory. Every prior paper works in the flash/offload regime, and most report speed, or energy from a model, rather than measured rails. That bound is new, but it is a predicted null, and the repo's RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH already registers it as its B=1 arm. The decode-locality claim as stated is not expected to hold.

For Ed's interest in weight edits, a sounder version is this: router or weight edits move energy only through (a) bytes touched per token (top-k edits, RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB, predicted at about +50 J per doubling), (b) the union of experts at B>1 (RQ-D-A03, ROUTING×BATCH), or (c) behavioural changes in output length. Per-token cache locality is not one of those channels on Apple silicon. Recommendation: merge ROUTER-EDIT into ROUTING×BATCH as its B=1 null-bound arm, and pair it with TOPK-KNOB as the positive control. Do not run it as a standalone decode-locality claim.

---

## 7. ENTROPY-DIAL: the data-dependent GPU power coefficient of Apple silicon (seat 1 (Fable))

### Fable 5.1 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Lucas & Juurlink, "ALUPower: Data Dependent Power Consumption in GPUs", IEEE MASCOTS 2016, doi:10.1109/MASCOTS.2016.21 (OPENED: open-access PDF via doi:10.14279/depositonce-7074, text extracted locally). Measured GTX 580 (Fermi) and GTX 750 Ti (Maxwell). FMUL kernel: all-zeros 155 W, random inputs +77 W (232 W), alternating all-zeros/all-ones 257 W. LFSR microbenchmark: energy almost perfectly linear in number of active (toggling) LFSRs; 0 vs 32 active = 28.8% (GTX 580) / 31.2% (GTX 750 Ti) of total energy. Per-instruction energy: FMUL 49.3-131.5 pJ (95% interval), IMUL 76.7-218.3 pJ. Fitted linear model in Hamming distance of consecutive operands HD(a0,a1), HD(b0,b1), HD(o0,o1), cross-operand HD, and POPC; RMS error 2.7 pJ (generated vectors), 7.8 pJ (Rodinia traces). Key for this idea: (i) adding exponent-value features to FADD/FMUL 'did not improve the prediction accuracy'; (ii) with all-zeros as baseline, some constant inputs on even warps use LESS energy than all-zeros (non-monotone in POPC), and effects depend strongly on register bank used; (iii) random vectors concentrate at medium Hamming distance, extremes are rare. This is the source of the idea's '155-257 W' number.
- Pekhimenko, Bolotin, Vijaykumar, Mutlu, Mowry, Keckler, "A Case for Toggle-Aware Compression for GPU Systems", HPCA 2016, doi:10.1109/HPCA.2016.7446064 (OPENED: PDF from users.ece.cmu.edu/~omutlu, text extracted locally). Establishes that bit toggles (0->1/1->0 switching of consecutive words) on on-chip and off-chip buses set the dynamic energy of GPU interconnect/memory channels; compression raises toggle count 20% to 2.2x across 242 GPU applications. Prior art for the 'Hamming distance of consecutive weight words' term applied to the memory-movement side of a GEMV (the side that dominates on a memory-bound Apple GEMV).
- Brier, Clavier, Olivier, "Correlation Power Analysis with a Leakage Model", CHES 2004, LNCS 3156 pp. 16-29 (OPENED: Springer abstract page). The Hamming-distance leakage model for CMOS power (power ~ bit flips relative to a reference state); two decades of side-channel work confirm data-dependent power is a universal CMOS property, so 'power depends on operand bits' is not itself a finding.
- UNVERIFIED (search budget for this session was exhausted after 0 successful searches; could not open): the idea's '38-67% in GEMM' datacenter-GPU span and any 2023-2026 random-vs-zero GEMM power papers on H100/A100; LPDDR4/LPDDR5 Data Bus Inversion (JEDEC) which would compress the toggle-count extremes on the Apple DRAM bus; any Apple-silicon data-dependent power study (none known to me; none found in the JouleWise repo).
- Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': NOT FOUND. Web search was unavailable this session; the only 'Astra' in the repo is a model seat name (docs/research_question_bank.md:1131 'New questions 2026-09-16 (Astra exploration)'), unrelated to weight editing. No repo file mentions GPT-6 or 'natively edit'. Treat as unverified until Ed supplies the link.
- JouleWise internal anchors used (canonical clone /Users/edr/code/CapstoneProject/jw-wt/handoff): docs/registrations/bytes_known_bytes_reading_rule_DRAFT.md (powermetrics has no DRAM rail; memory-chip energy lands in 'rest' = DC input minus rails; GPU-rail Delta resolved to +/-0.00054 J/GB on 0.067 J/GB in the worked example, cache K assumed 64 MiB); docs/registrations/block6_replication_DRAFT.md:712 (idle GPU rail 0.19 mW, sd 1.59 mW); docs/paper/draft-v2-skeleton.md:987 (gpu_power is an integer-mW estimate, gpu_energy integer mJ); man powermetrics ('Average power values reported by powermetrics are estimated and may be inaccurate').

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1) THE MECHANISM IS ALREADY ESTABLISHED, AND THE SPAN WILL EXIST. ALUPower (2016) showed ~30% of total GPU energy swings between zero-toggle and full-toggle operands on two NVIDIA generations, linear in Hamming distance, with per-op energies of tens of pJ. Apple GPUs are CMOS; operand isolation / clock gating in multipliers and bus toggling guarantee a measurable all-zero vs random span. Stage 0 will 'succeed' on the extreme fills almost regardless of the design, so a positive result there is confirmation, not discovery. 2) THE KERNEL IS MEMORY-BOUND, SO THE GPU RAIL SEES THE WRONG HALF OF THE EFFECT. A 4-bit or FP16 GEMV on M3 Max moves W bytes once per dispatch; at ~300-400 GB/s a 4096x4096 FP16 matrix (32 MB) takes ~80-100 us while its 33 MFLOP take ~2-3 us of ALU time. The data-dependent energy is therefore mostly (a) DRAM I/O and fabric toggling, which powermetrics does NOT report (no DRAM rail; it lands in 'rest' = DC meter minus rails), and (b) SRAM/register/dequant toggles on the GPU rail. The idea reads the GPU rail only; the DC meter must be the primary boundary, and the GPU-rail/rest split is itself the interesting output. LPDDR DBI (UNVERIFIED) may flatten the bus-toggle extremes, so the memory-side span may be much smaller than ALUPower's ALU-side numbers. 3) 'MONOTONE' IS CONTRADICTED BY THE ONLY DETAILED PRIOR STUDY. ALUPower found some constant inputs cheaper than all-zeros on even warps and large bank-dependent differences; the energy depends on the ORDER words meet the same lane/bank, i.e. on the kernel's thread mapping, not on matrix statistics alone. A coefficient fitted on one MLX kernel/version will not transfer to another kernel, a different tile shape, or a different dispatch order. 4) 'EXPONENT SPREAD' IS A WEAK PREDICTOR. ALUPower tried exponent features for FADD/FMUL and found no improvement; for integer 4-bit weights there is no exponent at all (only FP16 group scales). The three proposed statistics should be defined on the PACKED bytes as laid out in memory (nibble Hamming distance, scale-word toggles), not on float values. 5) THE 'PREDICT ANY WEIGHT EDIT' CLAIM IS VACUOUS BY CONSTRUCTION. Trained weights are ~Gaussian; mantissa and sign bits are ~0.5 toggle density, exponents span 6-8 values; zero fraction ~0. Realistic edits (rank-1 ROME/MEMIT updates, fine-tune deltas, activation-steering-equivalent weight patches) leave zero fraction, Hamming density and exponent spread essentially unchanged -- ALUPower explicitly notes random data concentrates at medium Hamming distance. The dial is pinned at the 'random' end for every edit that is not pruning or quantization (which are already studied as sparsity/quantization energy). The fitted coefficient will predict ~0 J for every edit of interest, which is correct but trivial. The only non-trivial prediction is the slope at the operating point, which must be measured with matched-statistic shuffles, not extreme fills. 6) DETECTABILITY IS FINE FOR EXTREMES, MARGINAL FOR REALISTIC EDITS. Replays repeat to ~2 J on 2-4 kJ (0.05-0.1%); GPU-rail contrasts resolve ~0.0005 J/GB on 0.067 J/GB. A 10-40% span on a ~10 W kernel (1-4 W) is >100x the floor. A realistic edit's effect, bounded by the medium-HD scatter in ALUPower (a few percent of ALU energy, times the ~5-10% ALU share of a memory-bound kernel), is ~0.1-0.5% of kernel power, i.e. 10-50 mW on 10 W: at or just above the 1-mW integer quantization and the loaded-rail DVFS/thermal jitter, and below it if the session is not quiet. The '1% of 10 W = 100 mW, 10x the KB floor' argument in the idea uses the IDLE rail sd (1.59 mW); the loaded floor is set by DVFS state changes and thermal drift, which are larger. 7) CONFOUNDS SPECIFIC TO THIS DESIGN. (a) Random BIT fills of FP16 produce NaN/Inf/subnormals; subnormal handling can change kernel time and ALU path, so fills must be random VALUES in a fixed exponent range, with time held constant and checked. (b) Apple GPU DVFS: lower ALU power may free headroom and raise GPU clock, changing power without changing data cost; log GPU HW active frequency from powermetrics and use it as a covariate, or pin the frequency by saturating the GPU with a constant companion load. (c) SLC: a fixed-shape matrix smaller than the system-level cache (worked example assumes K = 64 MiB) is served from SRAM on repeat dispatch, so the DRAM-toggle term vanishes; use matrices larger than SLC or a cyclic set of >K bytes. (d) Metal/MLX may not special-case zeros, but the hardware may (zero-gated multipliers), making 'zero fraction' a hardware-specific step rather than a continuous dial. (e) Activations and outputs also toggle (ALUPower includes HD of outputs); fix x and note the coefficient is for the pair (W-stats, x), not W alone. 8) PERFORMANCE SIDE. For a memory-bound GEMV, wall time is data-independent except via the NaN/subnormal confound, so the 'power AND performance' coupling Ed is interested in will be a null on performance unless the edit changes sparsity structure or the kernel branches on data. 9) RECOMMENDED REFRAME. Keep Stage 0 cheap (Air, DC meter + rails): (i) extreme fills to establish the Apple span and the GPU-rail vs rest partition; (ii) matched-statistic shuffles of a real trained matrix (same histogram, permuted order) to measure the order/toggle term separately from the value-histogram term; (iii) real LOCKIN-SWAP edited matrices as a bounded-null check with a preregistered upper limit. The honest publishable output is 'the energy footprint of surgical weight edits on Apple silicon is below X mW/J per dispatch, and here is why', plus the Apple toggle coefficient on the DC boundary.

**Residual novelty**

Not refuted as stated, but substantially pre-empted in mechanism. ALUPower (NVIDIA Fermi/Maxwell, 2016) already demonstrated a ~30% data-dependent span, the Hamming-distance linear model, the failure of exponent features, non-monotonicity, and bank/order dependence; Pekhimenko et al. (HPCA 2016) covers the bus-toggle side. What remains new: (1) no Apple-silicon calibration exists (UNVERIFIED negative, search unavailable); (2) the rails-vs-rest partition of toggle energy -- how much of the data-dependent cost shows on the powermetrics GPU rail versus in DRAM/fabric power visible only at the DC meter -- is a genuinely new measurement that only JouleWise's two-boundary setup can make, and it bears directly on Ed's worry about pulling power out of the noise; (3) a preregistered bounded null on realistic weight edits (ROME/MEMIT-style, fine-tune deltas) is new and useful for the interp audience, but the 'coefficient predicts any edit from weight statistics alone' framing should be dropped: by construction every realistic edit sits at the medium-Hamming operating point and the predicted effect is ~0. Reframe ENTROPY-DIAL as (a) Apple toggle coefficient on the DC boundary with rail partition, (b) order-vs-histogram decomposition via matched shuffles, (c) upper limit on edit energy footprint. Independently publishable as a short metrology note, not as 'ALUPower for Apple silicon' unless an Apple assembler-level microbenchmark (not available) is used.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Bhalachandra, Austin, Williams, Wright, 'Understanding the Impact of Input Entropy on FPU, CPU, and GPU Power', arXiv:2212.08805 (2022). OPENED (abstract plus ar5iv HTML). A100 DGEMM 16K x 16K: random inputs about 398 W vs fixed nonzero constants (2.0/0.5/1.0) about 239 W, +67%. Throughput was about 4% lower with random inputs. AMD EPYC 7763: +19% (188 vs 158 W). CPU memory power was unchanged (114.6 vs 114.9 W). No memory-bound or GEMV tests. Mechanism: bit flips between consecutive operands. The 155-257 W span quoted in the idea's detectability field does not appear in this paper and I could not trace it.
- Gregersen, Patel, Choukse, 'Input-Dependent Power Usage in GPUs', arXiv:2409.18324 (2024; Microsoft Research / UW). OPENED (abstract plus ar5iv HTML). A100 PCIe with CUTLASS GEMM in FP32, FP16, FP16 tensor-core and INT8, plus H100, V100 and RTX 6000. Power changes by up to about 38-40% with value distribution, bit similarity, sparsity and placement or sorting. Effects are non-monotone in places: power peaks around 30-40% sparsity with sorted inputs, and the bit-similarity trend is not fully consistent. Power was read from DCGM at 100 ms. No GEMV or memory-bound kernels; all-zero vs random was not measured.
- Taneja et al., 'Hot Pixels: Frequency, Power, and Temperature Attacks on GPUs and Arm SoCs', arXiv:2305.12784 (USENIX Security 2023). OPENED (ar5iv HTML, first 100k chars). M1/M2 CPU and integrated GPU read through IOReport. On the M1 GPU, a Hamming-weight sweep of a bitwise-AND kernel gave power-vs-Hamming-weight correlation -0.963 on a passively cooled MacBook Air: power DECREASES as Hamming weight rises because the chip throttles on heat. Actively cooled MacBook Pro and Mac Mini held maximum frequency (fdiv vs fmul: 10.7-12.1 W). M1 CPU with operands 0 vs 1: about 0.3 W (about 2.5%). Conclusion: data-dependent power on Apple silicon is visible in software counters, but how it appears depends on cooling.
- Wang, Paccagnella, Gang, Vasquez, Kohlbrenner, Shacham, Fletcher, 'GPU.zip: On the Side-Channel Implications of Hardware-Based Graphical Data Compression', IEEE S&P 2024. OPENED (hertzbleed.com/gpu.zip project page only). Preliminary results show data-dependent lossless compression on GPUs from AMD, Apple, Arm, Intel, Qualcomm and Nvidia. The page does not say whether general compute buffers (as opposed to textures or render targets) are compressed.
- Lucas & Juurlink, 'AluPower: Data Dependent Power Consumption in GPUs', IEEE MASCOTS 2016, pp. 95-104. UNVERIFIED: not opened; known only from Gregersen's citation, which says it measured single instructions such as FMUL and IMUL against how many bits are set.
- Pekhimenko et al., 'A Case for Toggle-Aware Compression for GPU Systems', HPCA 2016, pp. 188-200. UNVERIFIED: not opened; cited by Gregersen as the basis for the bit-flip hypothesis.
- JouleWise internal: the BYTES known-bytes (KB) attempt 3 result in /Users/edr/code/CapstoneProject/jw-wt/handoff/RUN_STATE.md (line ~922) and the reading rule in docs/registrations/bytes_known_bytes_reading_rule_DRAFT.md. DRAM-sourced minus cache-sourced energy: GPU rail 0.00879 +/- 0.00010 J/GB, rails 0.0092 +/- 0.0021, DC 0.0389 +/- 0.0072, rest 0.0296 +/- 0.0084 J/GB. powermetrics reports no DRAM channel.
- Ed's referenced 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': NOT FOUND. The search budget ran out before it could be searched, so I cannot confirm that it exists.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1) The extreme-fill effect is probably detectable, but the 10-40% span is optimistic for a memory-bound GEMV. The datacenter figures (38-67%) come from compute-bound GEMM, where about 40% of power is in the arithmetic units (Bhalachandra). In decode GEMV the arithmetic units are mostly idle: FP16 at about 350 GB/s is about 175 G weights/s, about 0.35 TFLOP/s, a small fraction of the M3 Max's peak. The data-dependent energy should therefore sit mainly in data movement. Our own KB run bounds that: the GPU rail carries about 0.0088 J/GB of memory-traffic energy, about 3 W at full bandwidth, even if 100% of it depended on the data. Add an arithmetic-unit share of a few hundred mW, plus more for 4-bit dequantization. A plausible all-zero vs random span on the GPU rail is roughly 0.3-3 W, perhaps 3-20% of GPU-rail power. That is still well above the GPU-rail resolution: 0.0001 J/GB at about 350 GB/s is about 35 mW. A correction: '1% of a 10 W kernel = 100 mW = 10x the KB floor' is really about 3x when the floor is converted at full bandwidth. 2) The boundary problem. Data-dependent DRAM I/O energy (LPDDR5 toggling, possibly damped by data-bus inversion) lands in DC 'rest', not in the rails. KB resolves rest only to about +/-28% (0.0296 +/- 0.0084 J/GB). So the memory-chip half of any entropy effect will be poorly resolved, and 'mean power' must be stated per boundary. 3) Sign and DVFS trap on the Air. Hot Pixels showed that on a passively cooled M1/M2 Air, higher Hamming weight lowered steady-state GPU power and frequency, because the chip throttles on heat. 'Stage 0 on the Air for shape and sign' can therefore give the wrong sign. Stage 0 should check frequency residency and either treat the result as energy per dispatch at matched frequency or skip the sign claim. Thermal drift and leakage between random and zero fills also have to be balanced by interleaving and by reporting temperature. 4) Compression and runtime confound. GPU.zip reports data-dependent hardware compression on Apple GPUs. If zero or low-entropy buffers are compressed, the effect becomes fewer bytes moved and a shorter runtime, not a 'toggle coefficient'. Report runtime and energy per dispatch, check that runtime is identical across fills, and treat any runtime change as a separate mechanism. 5) Monotonicity and choice of statistics. Prior work contradicts a monotone law (the non-monotone sparsity-with-sorting peak; the inconsistent bit-similarity trend). Toggles depend on the order the hardware actually reads (SIMD lane, threadgroup tile, the MLX 4-bit packing and per-group scale/bias layout), not on 'consecutive weight words' in logical row order. The fit can be badly specified even with a large effect. 'Exponent spread' does not exist for 4-bit integer nibbles, and all-zero 4-bit codes dequantize to the bias, not to zero. 6) The edit-prediction payoff is physically about zero. Rank-one or MEMIT-style surgical edits move FP16 weights by small relative amounts. That mostly flips low mantissa bits, which are already close to random, so zero fraction, Hamming distance and exponent spread barely change. The edits also touch a handful of matrices out of hundreds; under 4-bit requantization many edits barely move the codes. Predicted energy change per token for a real surgical edit is likely below 1e-4 of the run, orders of magnitude below the about 2 J per 2-4 kJ episode floor (about 0.05-0.1%). The coefficient is real, but 'validate on real edited matrices from LOCKIN-SWAP' is a null test. Only coarse edits that change weight statistics on purpose, such as large-fraction pruning, zero-ablation of many units, or bit-level quantization changes, could move power enough to measure, and the sparsity-lowers-power effect is already known on NVIDIA. 7) Scope. Prior-work coverage is incomplete: the web-search budget ran out after one query, so 2025-2026 Apple-specific or LLM-weight-entropy power work was not searched.

**Residual novelty**

Not refuted, but the novel part is much narrower than the pitch. Three things are already established: (a) GEMM power depends on input data on datacenter GPUs, through zero fraction, bit similarity, value placement and ordering (Bhalachandra 2022; Gregersen 2024; AluPower 2016 before them). (b) Apple M1/M2 GPU power, read through IOReport (the same counters powermetrics uses), follows operand Hamming weight in a simple bitwise kernel (Hot Pixels 2023). (c) Data moved through Apple GPUs can be compressed depending on its content (GPU.zip 2024). What none of the sources I opened covers: (1) a calibrated data-dependent power coefficient for memory-bound FP16 and MLX 4-bit GEMV, the kernel shape of LLM decode, on Apple silicon; (2) splitting that coefficient across boundaries (GPU rail vs DC 'rest', i.e. the memory chips and I/O); (3) the 4-bit case, where the statistics have to be defined on packed nibbles plus per-group scales and biases, not on FP16 words. That is a solid 'Apple-silicon port of the input-entropy power results' measurement note. It is not the claimed 'monotone law that predicts any weight edit'. Prior work already contradicts monotonicity: Gregersen found power peaks around 30-40% sparsity when inputs are sorted, and the bit-similarity trend was not consistent. Bhalachandra says energy tracks how values change from one row or column to the next, not the values themselves, so three summary statistics that ignore the order the hardware actually reads may not be enough. The most defensible deliverable is: the GPU-rail and DC-rest span between extreme fills for FP16 and 4-bit GEMV at fixed shape, plus a dose-response curve over zero fraction. Pitch it as a calibration and a bound, not as an edit predictor. Search caveat: the web-search budget ran out after one query, so 2025-2026 work (for example Apple-specific or LLM-weight-specific data-dependent power) was not searched and could still exist. The reference to 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights' could not be searched and was not found; I have no verified source for it.

---

## 8. DVFS-COUPLE: does weight entropy change tokens per second on the M3 Max? (seat 1 (Fable))

### Fable 5.1 checker: REFUTED

**Prior work**

- Taneja, Kim, Xu, van Schaik, Genkin, Yarom, 'Hot Pixels: Frequency, Power, and Temperature Attacks on GPUs and Arm SoCs', USENIX Security 2023, arXiv:2305.12784 (OPENED, full text via ar5iv). Directly answers the regime question. §4.1/§5.1 Tables 2 and 5: fan-cooled M1 MacBook Pro and Mac Mini are 'frequency constrained' -- they hold 3.2 GHz CPU / 1.28 GHz GPU regardless of instruction mix or data, and data-dependence appears only in power and temperature (e.g. GPU fdiv vs fmul on the Pro: 1.28/1.28 GHz, 12.1/10.7 W, 89/86 C). Only the passively cooled M1/M2 Air shows frequency coupling (CPU add-1 vs add-0: 2.85 GHz/11.7 W vs 2.88 GHz/12.0 W -- the '0.03 GHz at 12 W' figure the idea cites is this CPU result on an Air, not a GPU result and not a fan-cooled part; Air GPU fdiv/fmul 0.89/0.96 GHz). Appendix A: even on the thermally constrained Air, operand Hamming WEIGHT via the and instruction produced NO frequency or power correlation over 400-800 s averages; the authors conjecture ALU power differences are too small to move steady-state frequency and that only buffer bit-flips (Hamming distance) leak. Thermally constrained devices need very long settling (iGPU trace 7000 s; time-to-throttle 300-3000 s on M1).
- Wang, Paccagnella, He, Shacham, Fletcher, Kohlbrenner, 'Hertzbleed: Turning Power Side-Channel Attacks Into Remote Timing Attacks on x86', USENIX Security 2022, and the S&P 2023 follow-up 'DVFS Frequently Leaks Secrets' (hertzbleed.com OPENED; the S&P 2023 paper itself UNVERIFIED, only its listing was read). Establishes the mechanism the idea tests: data-dependent power becomes frequency/timing ONLY when the part is sitting at a power or thermal limit; otherwise DVFS does not react to operand data.
- Liu, Chakraborty, Chawla, Roggel, 'Frequency Throttling Side-Channel Attack', ACM CCS 2022, arXiv:2206.07012 (abstract OPENED). Same mechanism, independently: power management adjusts frequency only after a monitored electrical/thermal parameter crosses its threshold.
- Chawla, Liu, Chakraborty, Chervatyuk, Sun, Hamasaki, Kawakami, 'Uncovering Software-Based Power Side-Channel Attacks on Apple M1/M2 Systems', arXiv:2306.16391 v2 2024 (abstract OPENED). Data-dependent on-chip power readings on Apple silicon via SMC keys plus a feasibility study of a frequency-throttling channel on M1/M2 -- the Apple-specific power-vs-frequency separation the idea proposes to re-measure.
- GPU.zip (hertzbleed.com/gpu.zip OPENED; paper UNVERIFIED). Transparent, software-invisible data compression on integrated GPUs including Apple's. Relevant as a confound the design does not control: if any compute buffer path is compressed, operand entropy changes memory traffic and therefore time, independent of DVFS. Whether MTLBuffers used by MLX are ever compressed is UNVERIFIED.
- JouleWise repo, docs/research_question_bank.md lines 122-134 (P2-009 rich telemetry note): 'decode pins the GPU at 1380 MHz / idle_ratio 0.0 / ~22 W' on the M3 Max; the GPU freq_hz, dvfm_states residency and sw-requested-vs-achieved state are already parsed by joulewise/adapters/powermetrics.py (lines 2094-2130, active_freq_mhz_weighted). Also line 664, C5-1.4 'DVFS residency as a throttling early-warning' -- an already-banked question that subsumes the frequency-logging half of DVFS-COUPLE.
- Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': NOT FOUND. The session's web-search budget was exhausted before this could be searched, and I have no independent knowledge of such a model or capability. Treat as unverified; do not cite it in research docs until a primary source is located.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1. The null is pre-determined by regime, so the experiment as designed buys almost no information. Hot Pixels' mechanism (and Hertzbleed/CCS'22) requires the part to be at a power or thermal cap. The rig's own telemetry says sustained MLX decode pins the M3 Max GPU at 1380 MHz (M3 Max GPU max is ~1398 MHz -- UNVERIFIED spec, consistent with Hot Pixels' 1398 MHz figure for the M2 GPU) with idle_ratio 0.0 at only ~22 W GPU rail, plugged in, fans available. That is Hot Pixels' 'frequency constrained' class (their fan-cooled Pro/Mini never moved off max P-state for any instruction or data). Apple's GPU governor is utilization-driven and decode saturates utilization regardless of operand values, so there is no route from weight entropy to frequency unless a limiter engages. Expect exactly 0 MHz shift; the <1% bound is confirmed by construction, not by measurement. 2. Even where coupling exists, Hot Pixels Appendix A found operand Hamming weight alone produced no frequency effect; only Hamming distance (bit flips in buffers/datapath) did. ENTROPY-DIAL as a 'weight entropy' dial is a Hamming-weight-style manipulation unless it is specified as toggling density between consecutive operands (DRAM bus and register-file bit flips). Memory-bound decode's data-dependent power is mostly LPDDR5 I/O toggling, which Hot Pixels did not test, so the magnitude is open -- but it still cannot reach frequency on an uncapped part. 3. Detectability is not the bottleneck. powermetrics reports interval-averaged GPU freq in MHz plus dvfm_states residency at ~8.8 Hz (100 ms commanded); a 10-20 min fill yields ~5-10k samples, and a 1 MHz residency-weighted shift would be resolvable. tok/s repeatability on the rig is ~0.3-1.4% CV at the energy level (RQB lines 488-489), so a <1% tok/s bound needs interleaved ABAB arms with roughly n>=8 per arm, which is feasible but moot given point 1. 4. Design defects: (a) 'powermetrics dispatch rate' does not exist; the only throughput signal is mlx-lm tok/s or per-token timestamps from the runtime. (b) 10-20 min sustained runs introduce thermal drift that moves frequency far more than any entropy effect; thermal pressure level and fan state must be matched, and the comparison must be within-session interleaved, not sequential blocks. (c) Weight edits change output tokens, which changes decode length, stop position, and KV traffic; tok/s must be compared under forced/fixed-length decode with identical token streams or the latency arm measures the text, not the weights. (d) The repo's arm/monitor gates refuse any thermal pressure level != 0 (joulewise/hazards/thermal.py), so 'repeat near the thermal gate boundary' is not reachable under the frozen protocol without a deviation-log ruling. 5. Where the effect WOULD exist and the question becomes real: a passively cooled part (the M4 Air the project already owns, or an M3 Max in Low Power Mode / on battery / with fans suppressed), or a compute-bound prefill-heavy fill that drives GPU rail toward its limit. Hot Pixels' Air iGPU needed thousands of seconds to settle and leaked ~0.1 bit/s, so expect multi-hour settling and tiny effects. 6. Power-signal-noise framing (Ed's concern): frequency is immune to background-process noise only because it is pinned; on an uncapped part it carries no information. On a capped part, background load shares the same cap and becomes a direct confound in frequency too.

**Residual novelty**

Low as stated; moderate if re-aimed. What prior work has not done: (a) measure data-dependent frequency coupling under a real memory-bound LLM decode (DRAM-toggling power path) rather than synthetic ALU loops; (b) on M3/M4-class silicon; (c) tie it to weight EDITS rather than input data. All three only become testable where a limiter engages. Bank as DVFS-COUPLE-B: run the same ENTROPY-DIAL (specified as Hamming-DISTANCE / toggling density, not Hamming weight) on the fanless M4 Air after a documented thermal settle (>=30 min at load, log thermalpressurelevel and dvfm_states), interleaved ABAB with fixed-length forced decode and identical token streams, and on the M3 Max only in Low Power Mode or on battery as a positive-control arm. The M3 Max plugged-in arm should be kept only as a 20-minute negative control that confirms frequency pinning and lets the ENTROPY-DIAL energy result be interpreted as pure switching power with latency held fixed -- which is the useful outcome for the weight-edit interp question: on this rig energy and tok/s ARE separable, so edit-induced energy deltas are not confounded by DVFS. Note for the research docs: the '0.03 GHz at 12 W' anchor is an M1 Air CPU result, not a GPU result; and the 'GPT-6 Astra weight editing' reference could not be located and must stay unverified.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- OPENED: Taneja, Kim, Xu, van Schaik, Genkin, Yarom, 'Hot Pixels: Frequency, Power, and Temperature Attacks on GPUs and ARM SoCs', arXiv:2305.12784 (2023). The USENIX Security 2023 venue comes from a search snippet of the sec23 slides URL and is UNVERIFIED. This is the closest prior work, and it largely predicts the null. Passively cooled M1 Air parts are thermally constrained, so data and instruction differences show up in frequency. Actively cooled M1 MacBook Pro and Mac mini are 'usually frequency constrained': they hold 3.2 GHz CPU and 1.28 GHz iGPU, and leakage moves into power and temperature. The paper states the M1 'does not exhibit instruction- or data-dependent frequency when it is actively cooled'. GPU fdiv vs fmul on MBP/mini: 1.28 GHz both, power 12.1 vs 10.7 W. Data-dependent iGPU results (Hamming weight and distance, r about -0.95 to -0.97 vs frequency and power) are given only for the M1 Air, with no absolute watts. Time-to-throttle on M1 CPU is about 300-3000 s. Readings came from IOReport via SocPowerBuddy at 10 ms; powermetrics was not used.
- OPENED: Gregersen, Patel, Choukse, 'Input-Dependent Power Usage in GPUs', arXiv:2409.18324 (2024; read via the abstract and ar5iv). On an A100 running CUTLASS GEMM, input patterns change power by up to 38% (sparsity, few unique values, sorting, bit similarity). Matrix size was chosen as 'the largest power of two that did not consistently throttle', and iteration runtime was 'consistent to a microsecond-level'. So the paper shows data-dependent power with no runtime coupling when the GPU is not power-limited, but it avoided the power-limited regime on purpose and did not test Apple or memory-bound decode.
- OPENED: Wang, Paccagnella, He, Shacham, Fletcher, Kohlbrenner, 'Hertzbleed: Turning Power Side-Channel Attacks Into Remote Timing Attacks on x86', USENIX Security 2022 (hertzbleed.com). Data-dependent power becomes data-dependent frequency and wall time under DVFS on Intel and AMD CPUs. Arm is listed as 'may be affected'. This is the canonical precedent for the claim's 'or it does' branch, but on CPUs.
- OPENED (project page only): Wang et al., 'GPU.zip: On the Side-Channel Implications of Hardware-Based Graphical Data Compression', IEEE S&P 2024, which includes Apple iGPUs. Hardware compression makes memory traffic data-dependent. The page does not say whether compute buffers such as MLX weight tensors are compressed (UNVERIFIED either way). If they were, weight entropy would change bytes moved, and so decode tok/s, through a path that has nothing to do with DVFS. That is worth one line in the design as an alternative mechanism.
- NOT FOUND: Ed's 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights' reference. I could not check it: this session's web-search budget ran out on the first attempt, and I did not work around the limit. I make no claim that it exists or does not exist.
- Repo context (read-only): the rig is an M3 Max 128 GB on a 140 W adapter, with 10 Hz powermetrics. joulewise/adapters/powermetrics.py already parses GPU dvfm_states and active_freq_mhz_weighted. docs/process_traces/2026-10-27-magistrate-a46602ae/brief-thermal70-r2.md shows Llama-3.3-70B sustained decode producing OS thermal-pressure level-1 episodes of 10-35 s, with onset 10-25 s after request end. So the rig does approach a thermal boundary on large models.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1. Hot Pixels already predicts the null. The M3 Max MacBook Pro is actively cooled, and Hot Pixels found actively cooled M1 MacBook Pro and Mac mini parts frequency-pinned (CPU and GPU at max P-state, no data-dependent frequency). On those parts, extra switching power goes into temperature and then fan speed, not into frequency. The M3 Max draws more power than an M1, so this needs checking, but the default expectation is a GPU-frequency shift of 0. A null result therefore adds little; only a positive result would be news.

2. The detectability anchor is misattributed. The '0.03 GHz on an M1 Air at 12 W' figure is CPU data (all-ones vs zero add: 2.85 vs 2.88 GHz, 11.7 vs 12.0 W) on a passively cooled, thermally constrained machine.
