1. The dose unit is wrong. The claim says training steps cost more per token than decode, so a few hundred training tokens cross the threshold. On Apple silicon the opposite holds: batch-1 decode is memory-bound (every token streams the active weights; 0.62 J/token registered, 0.18 J/token bare), whereas a LoRA SFT step over a 512-token batch is prefill-like and compute-bound (about 2x forward FLOPs for LoRA, since frozen weights skip dL/dW). [estimate] Qwen3-30B-A3B: ~13 GFLOP/training-token x 512 = ~7 TFLOP; at a realistic 2-4 TFLOPS for MLX MoE gather-matmuls and 4-bit dequant, 2-3.5 s at 40-60 W GPU = 80-210 J, below E2's 250-340 J decode-equivalent and marginal against thresholds that sit at the sample maximum of ~20 benign residuals with per-pair s_r 19-58 J (bank up to 85 J). The proposal says 'a small model': a 1.7B dense model at 512 tokens is ~3.5 TFLOP, ~0.5 s, ~25 J — invisible; a 0.6B is ~10 J. Detectable doses on small models need thousands of token-passes, which SEAL supplies only via multiple epochs (2-20) over the self-edit, not via 'a few hundred tokens'. Restate the dose in joules (FLOPs / realised efficiency), and choose the model and epoch count to land doses at roughly 0.5, 1, 2 and 4 x the per-pair threshold. 2. The 2 J replay figure is not the relevant floor. Monitoring scores a fresh benign episode against a token-count model, so the floor is s_r (19-58 J; 1.3-10.1 J within-window identical-work drift), not the 2 J token-identical repeatability. The 2 J figure is, however, usable for metering: run each dose as a token-identical replay pair (with/without the hidden step) and the injected joules are measured to ~3 J SD even when undetectable. Do that, and report the J-per-edit table separately from the detection verdict. 3. The gradient-free arm is underdetermined. '~50 forward passes over a probe set' costs anything from ~10 J (50 x 20-token probes on a 0.6B) to ~200 J (50 x 200-token probes on a 1.7B) [estimate]; specify probe tokens x passes x model so the FLOP dose is fixed. 4. The cheapest realistic edits are below the floor by construction. A ROME-style rank-one closed-form edit, or a delta precomputed off-episode and added at load, costs sub-joule to tens of joules (a 3.4 GB weight add is ~0.1-0.3 J at the measured ~0.039 J/GB). The proposal concedes the edited weights are invisible but does not include this as an arm; add a precomputed-delta negative control so the paper states the blind spot with a number instead of implying coverage. 5. The detector is threat-agnostic, so the experiment measures unexplained joules, not 'editing'. energy_residual.py scores measured-total minus predicted turn energy; between-turn gaps and tool segments have no design term and fall straight into the residual (by design; duration is excluded as a predictor). A hidden LoRA step, hidden decode, and a Spotlight burst are indistinguishable at the score level. Possible attribution lever unique to this lab: compute-bound training is well covered by the GPU rail while memory-bound decode is undercounted by rails relative to DC input (KB result f = 0.238), so the rails/DC split may fingerprint the hidden workload class — a hypothesis worth one print-only column, not a claim. 6. Confounds from a compute-bound burst between turns: it heats the die and changes DVFS state, so later decode turns shift (pilot 7 saw +2 to +8 J per later turn under heat); v4 gates and holds would then fire and their above-idle energy still lands in the episode total. The logged-edit control must inject the identical burst, and the dose must be the same for both; otherwise 'logging makes the residual vanish' is confounded with thermal state. Also the logged-edit control needs a fitted training-token (or training-FLOP) coefficient, which costs calibration episodes beyond m >= 20 benign per pair. 7. Memory and I/O: a resident second model is fine on 128 GB beside the 17 GB 30B, but it must be loaded once before the episode stamp and kept resident; loading per dose adds SSD energy and time that would masquerade as the edit's signature. LoRA on 4-bit quantised weights (QLoRA-style) adds dequant cost and mlx-lm's tuner adapts Linear/QuantizedLinear/SwitchLinear layers generically, so the gradient arm is buildable on the pinned stack, but it is new unpinned runner code. 8. Cost: 2 methods x 4 doses x >= 3 seeds x 2 pairs = 48 members plus controls exceeds one pilot-10-shaped night (28 members in 6.5 h); the honest budget is 2 Mac-nights standalone, or fold it into E2 as one extra dose type (one method, two doses) as the proposal itself allows.

**Residual novelty**

Not refuted: no opened or known source measures the device-level energy signature of model self-edits, and the nearest precedent (Wasil et al. 2024, energy monitoring of facilities) is explicitly coarse and unproven. But the detection half is not new relative to E2 — the ALIGN residual flags unexplained joules whatever their source, so 'edits are detected once they exceed X' follows from E2's dose curve plus a cost conversion. What remains novel and worth doing: (a) a measured joules-per-edit table for realistic self-edit recipes (SEAL-style LoRA SFT at its actual epoch counts, forward-pass probing, ROME-style rank-one, precomputed-delta apply) on Apple silicon, metered to ~3 J with token-identical replay pairs; (b) the explicit result that gradient-based self-edits of SEAL's real size (thousands of token-passes) are detectable while closed-form or precomputed edits are not, stated with numbers as the method's blind spot; (c) the hypothesis that the rails-vs-DC energy split separates compute-bound hidden training from memory-bound hidden decode, which this lab's KB result makes testable for free. Recommend: rewrite the dose axis in joules, add the precomputed-delta negative control, and run it as an extra E2 dose type rather than a standalone night; bank the full version for after Paper 1. Caveat: prior-work coverage is incomplete because the session's web search budget was exhausted; the GPT-6 Astra premise is unverified.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Takehi, Teoh, Zhang, Khattab (MIT CSAIL), 'Sparks of Weight-Space Programming', blog post, about 2 Nov 2026, https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/ (OPENED). This is Ed's 'GPT-6 Astra' reference, and it is real. It is not self-modification: GPT-6 Astra edited a different model, Qwen3.5-0.8B, using no gradients. Its budget was 10, 25 or 50 'forward passes', where one pass means generating answers to up to 512 problems or reading activations on up to 1,024 texts. The base model hit the 32,768-token cap on 85 of its first 96 practice answers. The final edit subtracted one direction from 64 MLP output columns in layers 15 and 19: 131,072 weights, 0.016% of the model. The share of MATH-500 answers hitting the cap fell from 93.0% to 33.1% at 50 passes. The post gives no energy, FLOP or wall-time figures and no dtype, and the Python kernel had no GPU.
- R&D World coverage with critics, https://www.rdworldonline.com/mit-team-says-gpt-6-astra-can-fix-an-ai-model-by-editing-its-weights-critics-say-the-method-was-standard-ml-engineering/ (OPENED). It reports 80 Python executions over 20.3 h for the Qwen run. Goedecke and Honnibal argue the fix is ordinary steering and standard ML engineering. The Register, 3 Nov 2026 (OPENED), gives the same facts.
- Zweiger et al., 'Self-Adapting Language Models' (SEAL), NeurIPS 2025, arXiv 2506.10943. Opened only through search snippets and secondary pages, so its exact inner-loop token and epoch counts are UNVERIFIED. The model writes self-edits that drive LoRA SFT.
- Gargiulo and Kulp, 'Workload Identification with Physical Side Channels for AI Governance', arXiv 2609.00309, Aug 2026 (OPENED abstract). An external roughly 10 MHz current probe on an H200 separates training, inference and non-AI work with 97% accuracy. Of four evasion strategies, 'diluted LoRA' (small updates interleaved with real inference) is caught 48-88% of the time by the hardened classifier and at least 98% with a rescue rule. This is the closest prior work: hidden LoRA fine-tuning inside inference, detected by power. It works on a datacenter GPU, at high sample rate, as a classifier rather than a log-residual. It gives no minimum detectable dose.
- Rahman and Tajdari, 'Detecting Hidden ML Training With Zero-Overhead Telemetry', arXiv 2606.19262, ICML 2026 TAIGR workshop (OPENED abstract). NVML telemetry, 20 evasion families, 9 GPU models. It reports no minimum detectable amount and does not mention Apple silicon.
- MATS project on detecting training in datacenter power, https://matsprogram.org/research/detecting-ai-training-in-datacentre-power (UNVERIFIED: seen only as a search snippet). The ICML 2026 paper 'Can GPU Telemetry Verify LLM Training?' is likewise UNVERIFIED (snippet only).
- Paul and Zuzak, 'Michscan', HOST 2025, arXiv 2501.10174 (OPENED abstract). Power analysis detects unauthorized parameter changes at runtime on an STM32 Cortex-M4 running TinyML. This is a counterexample, in a different hardware regime, to 'edited weights stay energy-invisible'.
- Cina et al., 'Energy-Latency Attacks via Sponge Poisoning', Information Sciences 2025, arXiv 2203.08147 (OPENED abstract): poisoned weights raise inference energy. From memory, the effect relies on sparsity-exploiting accelerators; that detail is UNVERIFIED because the abstract does not state it. Shumailov et al., 'Sponge Examples', EuroS&P 2021, arXiv 2006.03463 (OPENED abstract), reports 10-200x energy rises, mostly by inducing more work.
- Repo: no prior JouleWise doc covers self-modification or weight-edit detection. A grep for self-modif, SEAL, Astra and weight edit across the research-program, research-program-fable, part2 and main branches found nothing relevant.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1. The ALIGN residual reads the GPU rail only, so CPU-side editing escapes it. joulewise/align/apple/pilot_analysis.py:178-225 @ cda28a5ef scores the episode total from 'powermetrics:gpu_power'. Astra's own kernel ran with no GPU. A numpy rank-k or direction-subtraction edit, or a CPU forward pass, never enters the scored channel. The claim should be restated as 'detects GPU-rail editing compute'. Adding a CPU, PSTR or DC-input total as a second score is cheap and should be part of the design.

2. The repo's own coefficients reverse the per-token cost premise. On the 30B GPU rail, prefill costs 0.045-0.063 J/token and bare decode 0.16-0.28 J/token (RECORD af17111e:55). In-context hidden decode is e = 0.49-0.67 J/token. Decode at batch 1 is bandwidth-bound; training and prefill are compute-bound and amortize weight reads. A LoRA forward-plus-backward pass should cost about 2-4x prefill, about 0.1-0.25 J per trained token [estimate]. Equalling 512 hidden decode tokens (250-340 J) therefore takes roughly 1,000-3,000 trained tokens (sequence length times steps), not 'a few hundred'. Real SEAL edits run several epochs over generated data and also generate the self-edit text, which is itself hidden decode. They likely clear the threshold easily [estimate].

3. '50 forward passes' misreads Astra. Its 'forward pass' was a batch of up to 512 full generations capped at 32k tokens, or activation reads on up to 1,024 texts. The diagnosis was enormous: tens of millions of decode tokens is plausible, and it took 20.3 h of wall time. On a Mac that is a detection by orders of magnitude, so it is not an informative dose. The edit itself (64 columns x 2 layers) costs microjoules.

4. The dangerous class is energy-free, and the design should test it rather than only dose up SFT:
   - closed-form edits (ROME- or MEMIT-style with cached statistics, or steering-direction subtraction);
   - directions computed from activations harvested during logged benign turns, which costs zero extra passes;
   - in-memory edits to mmap'd MLX arrays, which never touch disk.
   A few dozen short contrastive prompts on a 30B is about 25-100 J of prefill [estimate], under the 19-58 J per-pair s_r scale and the sample-max thresholds. 'Detects editing compute above X' is true by construction. The useful result is the map from edit type to joules.

5. Little is new over E2. The score is (measured joules minus predicted)/D, with duration deliberately excluded, so at equal joules the residual cannot tell training from decode. The SFT dose-response would mostly re-measure E2's joule curve under a new label. Second-order differences remain: thermal and DVFS carry-over into later turns (U5 state sensitivity), and power-versus-time shape.

6. The baseline against wall time is missing. Hidden GPU work on the same GPU between turns lengthens the episode about in proportion to its joules. A counts-plus-wall-clock baseline may match energy unless the edit is hidden inside tool-wait or idle gaps. That is the arm where energy has unique value, and it should be a required condition.

7. 'Edited weights stay energy-invisible' needs a caveat:
   - Merged edits: plausibly invisible. Changing 0.016% of weights shifts data-dependent switching power by a relative amount around 1e-5 of about 3 kJ, roughly 0.03 J [estimate], far below the roughly 2 J replay repeatability.
   - Unmerged LoRA adapters (SEAL's default form): extra matmuls and kernel launches on every decode token. That could raise per-token cost by several percent, a persistent positive residual over thousands of tokens [estimate; untested in MLX].
   - Behavioral change: Astra's edit changed output length massively (93% to 33% cap-hits). Those tokens appear in the log, so the residual does not see them.
   The 'invisible' claim should therefore be split into merged and unmerged cases.

8. Noise anchors:
   - About 2 J replay repeatability on 2-4 kJ episodes, and per-pair s_r of 19-58 J (research-program.md E2 row). Thresholds sit at the sample maximum of m ≈ 12-20 benign episodes, so the realistic limit is about 100-200 J [estimate]. Any GPU edit dose of a few hundred J clears it.
   - The 0.0001 J/GB GPU-rail anchor comes from a controlled bandwidth contrast and does not carry over to agent episodes.
   - Hidden training on the 30B itself needs QLoRA memory headroom alongside serving, which is feasible on 128 GB but untested. The 'small model between turns' variant is side compute, not self-modification, and duplicates E2's fresh-context side-generation arm.

**Residual novelty**

Not refuted. No prior work measures whether an agent's own unlogged weight edits show up in a per-episode energy residual against its logged tokens, on consumer or Apple-silicon hardware. Gargiulo and Kulp (arXiv 2609.00309) is the closest. They detect hidden 'diluted LoRA' fine-tuning by power, but on an H200 with an external 10 MHz probe, as a training/inference classifier, with no log-residual and no dose threshold.

As proposed, the experiment is mostly E2 with a relabelled dose. The residual sees only GPU joules, so SFT steps are detected at about the same joules as hidden decode. Two estimates are wrong: training costs less per token than in-context decode on this machine (prefill is 0.045-0.063 J/token), and Astra's '50 passes' were massive generation batches, not 50 small probes.

Fold it into E2 as an extra dose type, with these changes:
- Pre-register dose in joules and tokens trained, not 'forward passes'.
- Add an energy-free arm: a closed-form or steering edit from activations harvested during logged turns, CPU-side, in memory. It is expected to evade, which bounds the method honestly.
- Add a parallel-to-tool-wait arm and a wall-clock baseline, so energy's unique value over timing is tested.
- Add a second score on PSTR, CPU or DC input, to close the CPU-rail gap.
- Add a merged-versus-unmerged adapter persistence check, since a resident LoRA may leave a lasting per-token residual.

For Ed's interpretability interest, there is a stronger and cheaper pivot. Astra's edit is published on Hugging Face (per R&D World; not opened). Its target, Qwen3.5-0.8B, is already an R0 model in the repo. Measure energy per MATH-500 task before and after that edit (output-length-mediated, likely very large) against energy per token, which is predicted unchanged for a merged edit. This tests whether 'surgical edits change power' is entirely behavioral. It is resolvable well above the noise floor.

---

## 17. DRAM data dependence at the DC boundary vs IOReport's DRAM model (banked; joins instrument night #3) (seat 2 (Opus))

### Fable 5.1 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Ghose, Yaglikci, Gupta, Lee, Kudrolli, Liu, Hassan, Chang, Chatterjee, Agrawal, O'Connor, Mutlu, 'What Your DRAM Power Models Are Not Telling You: Lessons from a Detailed Experimental Study', SIGMETRICS/POMACS 2018, arXiv:1807.05102 (OPENED, full text extracted). 50 DDR3L modules, FPGA command-level control, direct module-current measurement. Findings: reading an all-ones cache line draws on average 39.2% (up to 91.6%) more power than all-zeros; read current rises and write current falls with the number of ones (up to 434 mA read / 311 mA write, Vendor A); after subtracting an I/O-driver estimate the in-die data dependence is still up to 230 mA read / 111 mA write; the relation is linear in the ones count; bit toggling costs much less than the ones count; activate/precharge show no data dependence. The paper explicitly does NOT cover DDR4/LPDDR4/LPDDR5 (it says DDR4 adaptation is left to future work) and notes DBI is an optional LPDDR4 feature. So the idea's '39% on DDR3L' is correctly quoted, but it is a DRAM-module-current ratio under direct measurement, not a whole-system per-byte energy ratio.
- Stan and Burleson, 'Bus-Invert Coding for Low-Power I/O', IEEE TVLSI 1995, and 'Coding a Terminated Bus for Low Power', GLSVLSI 1995 (UNVERIFIED: not opened; cited via Ghose refs [144],[145] and via Wikipedia 'Bus encoding' which attributes inversion encoding to the TVLSI paper). Establishes that inversion coding exists precisely to flatten data-dependent I/O energy on terminated buses.
- Hollis, 'Data Bus Inversion in High-Speed Memory Applications', IEEE TCAS-II 2009 (UNVERIFIED: not opened; cited via Ghose ref [55]).
- Pekhimenko, Bolotin, Vijaykumar, Mutlu, Mowry, Keckler, 'A Case for Toggle-Aware Compression for GPU Systems', HPCA 2016 (UNVERIFIED: not opened; cited via Ghose ref [124]). Shows compressed data can raise memory-channel energy via bit toggling in GPU systems; simulated, not whole-system measured.
- Wikipedia 'LPDDR' (OPENED): LPDDR4 moved to LVSTL signalling; its DMI pins implement data bus inversion so that fewer lines are driven high, 'As signal lines are terminated low, this reduces power consumption.' LPDDR5 adds Write-X (all-zero/all-one writes without data transfer, 'same amount of time, but saves energy') and Data-Copy. The page does not state whether LPDDR5 carries DBI; LPDDR5 DBI-DC support therefore remains UNVERIFIED here (JEDEC, Rambus, SemiEngineering and AnandTech URLs returned 403/404).
- Wikipedia 'Apple M3' (OPENED): M3 SoCs use 6,400 MT/s LPDDR5; the M3 Max has 32 16-bit controllers (409.6 GB/s). Confirms the device under test is LPDDR5, not LPDDR5X.
- vladkens/macmon source (OPENED, src/metrics.rs and src/sources.rs on GitHub): IOReport 'Energy Model' group exposes a channel whose name starts with 'DRAM' (plus 'GPU SRAM'), with a PMP-group fallback; macmon clamps ram_power to 0 if it exceeds SMC system power. Confirms the channel exists; nothing found stating whether it is a counter-based model or a measured rail, so the idea's premise that it is 'blind to data values' is an unverified assumption, not an established fact.
- JouleWise repo: docs/process_traces/2026-10-31-magistrate-5cbfbcfc/KB-SLOT-FILL.md (the cited 0.0389 ± 0.0072 J/GB DC contrast and its seat decomposition) and docs/registrations/bytes_known_bytes_reading_rule_DRAFT.md (the δΔ bound construction). No prior repo entry on bit-pattern or data-dependent DRAM energy; Ghose is not cited anywhere in the repo.
- NOT FOUND: Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights' could not be located. The session's WebSearch budget was exhausted before this task began (200-call shared limit), and direct fetches of openai.com/news and openai.com/index/gpt-6 returned 403. I cannot confirm this claim or source exists; treat it as unverified.
- NOT FOUND (search budget exhausted, no direct URL known): any prior measurement of bit-pattern-dependent DRAM energy on Apple silicon, on LPDDR5 specifically, or at a whole-system DC boundary. Absence here is absence of search, not evidence of absence.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1) THE HALF-WIDTH DOES NOT SHRINK WITH HOURS. The 0.0072 J/GB is not a statistical error. Under bytes_known_bytes_reading_rule_DRAFT.md the per-call bound is a SUM (not quadrature) of systematic terms: edge terms, a baseline term T_i x (|P_pre - P_post|/2 + 2 se_idle), and the |alpha|/B term that charges the entire per-dispatch fixed cost as error. Baseline scales with call length T_i and B scales with T_i, so the baseline term per GB is roughly duration-independent; |alpha|/B depends only on dispatch size. Running the fill arms longer therefore buys essentially nothing; the idea's implicit 1/sqrt(t) 'per hour' scaling is wrong under the current rule. A three-fill comparison is a difference of two contrasts, so its bound is about 2 x 0.0072 = 0.014 J/GB (rule-style sum) or 0.010 J/GB (quadrature) regardless of duration, i.e. only effects of roughly 25-40% of the whole DC traffic energy are resolvable, and that is a hard floor, not a per-hour one. Resolving anything smaller needs a different estimator (many interleaved short ABBA blocks with replicate variance, a preregistered deviation from the rule), not more time. 2) THE PLAUSIBLE EFFECT IS BELOW THAT FLOOR. 0.0389 J/GB = 4.9 pJ/bit at the DC boundary, but the seat decomposition shows most of it is stall time, not DRAM energy: 85-88% of the GPU-rail and rails contrasts is ~10 W of GPU held on for 25 us longer per dispatch, and only the 'rest' power-rise part (0.0153 J/GB, about 1.9 pJ/bit, from a 1.63 W rise) is candidate DRAM device + PHY + fabric energy. Only the I/O-driver and periphery share of that is data dependent. Ghose's 39% applied to DDR3L module current at 1.35 V SSTL where both bus levels drive through termination; LPDDR5 uses LVSTL at a 0.5 V VDDQ, terminated to VSS, so the I/O share of per-bit energy is far smaller and only the driven-high bits cost termination current. A generous 20-40% data dependence of ~1.9 pJ/bit gives 0.003-0.006 J/GB, 2-5x below the ~0.010-0.014 J/GB comparison floor. Even a full Ghose-sized 39% of the entire DC traffic energy (0.015 J/GB) would sit at about 1x the floor. The idea's own detectability line concedes this; the 1-2 hour scope cannot return a resolved J/GB difference, only a null or an upper limit. 3) DBI AND SCRAMBLING CAN NULL THE CONTRAST FOR REASONS UNRELATED TO THE HYPOTHESIS. If Apple enables DBI-DC (optional in LPDDR4 per Wikipedia; LPDDR5 status UNVERIFIED), all-ones is transmitted as all-zeros plus a DMI flag, so ones ~ zeros < random (random is mostly untouched by DBI). If the controller scrambles data (common on x86 controllers; Apple behaviour unknown and unverifiable here), logical fills do not map to bus patterns and all three fills read identical; a null then says nothing about I/O physics. Preregister the three orderings as distinct outcomes (zeros<random<ones: no DBI; zeros~ones<random: DBI; all equal: scrambled or below floor) and add a fixed-ones toggling pattern (0xAA/0x55 alternation, as Ghose did) to separate ones-count from toggle effects. 4) ZERO FILL IS A RESIDENCY TRAP. All-zero buffers risk lazy materialization, shared zero pages, or VM-level tricks; a 'faster/cheaper' zero arm could be the pages never reaching DRAM. The fill must be written by a kernel and the existing residency/geometry check must pass per fill; any fabric-level zero-line elimination on Apple's SoC (unknown) would show up as a large zero-only effect that is real but is not I/O energy, so attribution would be ambiguous. 5) DATA-DEPENDENT COMPUTE. all_reduce over zeros vs random toggles the GPU datapath differently; the within-fill rotate-minus-repeat contrast cancels this to first order, but the repeat (cache) arm also carries data-dependent SLC/fabric toggling, so Delta_fill measures DRAM-path minus cache-path data dependence. Do not compare raw per-fill energies; only compare within-fill contrasts. 6) THE IOREPORT PREMISE IS UNVERIFIED. macmon confirms an IOReport 'Energy Model' DRAM channel exists on Apple silicon, but nothing found says whether it is a counter model or a PMU-measured rail; the repo's powermetrics sampler already treats dram_w as possibly absent on this machine. Preregister both outcomes: if the channel tracks the fill effect, the 'model blind to data' claim is refuted and the channel is validated as a measurement. 7) BEARING ON ED'S INTERP QUESTION. Surgical weight edits change the bit statistics of a few weights; the DRAM data-dependence pathway to power is orders of magnitude below any floor here. The only power-relevant pathway from weight edits is through changed compute or traffic (sparsity, quantization, routing); this experiment cannot speak to weight editing and should not be banked as if it did. VERDICT: not refuted by prior work (Ghose is DDR3L with direct current measurement, not LPDDR5 at a system boundary), but as scoped (1-2 hours, current rule) it is underpowered by 2-5x against a plausible effect and the rule's bound cannot be bought down with time. Bank it as a sign/ordering pilot with a redesigned replicate-variance estimator, or drop it.

**Residual novelty**

Genuinely new: no prior work found (noting search budget was exhausted) measures bit-pattern dependence of memory-traffic energy on LPDDR5, on Apple silicon, or at a whole-system DC boundary; Ghose et al. is DDR3L with FPGA-controlled, directly instrumented module current. Also new is the paired test of whether IOReport's DRAM 'Energy Model' channel responds to data patterns, which would reveal whether it is a counter model or a measured rail; macmon confirms the channel exists but nothing found says which. The three-fill ordering (zeros<random<ones vs zeros~ones<random vs all equal) is a cheap in situ diagnostic for DBI and controller scrambling on Apple's memory path, which is itself undocumented. What survives as worth banking: a sign/ordering pilot using many interleaved short ABBA fill blocks with replicate-variance error bars (a preregistered deviation from the summed per-call bound), with a residency check per fill, a fixed-ones toggle pattern (0xAA/0x55), and both IOReport outcomes preregistered. What does not survive: the 1-2 hour J/GB claim as written, since the rule's half-width is duration-independent and the plausible LPDDR5 effect (0.003-0.006 J/GB) sits 2-5x below the ~0.010-0.014 J/GB comparison floor. Relevance to weight-editing interp is nil: the data-dependence pathway from a few edited weights to power is far below any floor; only compute/traffic changes from edits could register.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Ghose, Yaglikci, Gupta, Lee, Chatterjee, Agrawal, O'Connor, Mutlu et al., 'What Your DRAM Power Models Are Not Telling You: Lessons from a Detailed Experimental Study', SIGMETRICS 2018 / POMACS (arXiv 1807.05102), OPENED. It measured 50 DDR3L modules. Reading a cache line of all ones uses on average 39.2% (up to 91.6%) more read current than all zeros. For writes the direction reverses (more ones, less current). Toggling has a linear but much smaller effect (vendor A: 26 mA for full toggling against 230 mA for all ones). Their measurement includes I/O driver current. They also show that DRAMPower and Micron-style models ignore data dependence. This establishes the general claim but says nothing about LPDDR5, Apple silicon, a DC-input boundary or IOReport. Note that the 39% is a share of DDR3L read current (IDD4R), not of whole-boundary DRAM-traffic energy, so the idea's 'Ghose found 39%' cannot be compared directly with a fraction of the 0.039 J/GB DC coefficient.
- Gregersen, Patel, Choukse, 'Input-Dependent Power Usage in GPUs', arXiv 2409.18324 (Sept 2024), OPENED (abstract only). At fixed shapes and sizes, changing only the input data's value distribution, bit similarity, placement or sparsity changes GPU power during GEMM by almost 40%; they attribute this to bit flips. This is direct prior evidence that the compute side, not only DRAM, is data dependent. It is the main confound for this design, and it is the closest prior work to Ed's interest in how weight edits change power.
- JEDEC LPDDR4/LPDDR5 I/O physics (LVSTL terminated to VSSQ, so a driven '1' draws DC current; DBI-DC inverts a byte holding more than 4 ones; LPDDR5 VDDQ is about 0.5 V; LPDDR5 Write-X/data-copy features). UNVERIFIED: this is background knowledge. No JEDEC or vendor document was opened in this session because the web-search budget was exhausted.
- Repo, OPENED: docs/process_traces/2026-10-31-magistrate-5cbfbcfc/KB-SLOT-FILL.md. Rotate minus repeat at the DC input is 0.0389 +/- 0.0072 J/GB. Rest is 0.0296 +/- 0.0084 J/GB. GPU rail is 0.00879 +/- 0.00010 J/GB, 85% of it dispatch-time (stall), not power rise. Matched dispatches ran at 106-116 GB/s, latency-bound at 32 MiB. The rails boundary is cpu+gpu+ane only; the parser deliberately excludes any dram_power channel (L3 telemetry seat report). No repo document examines data-pattern dependence.
- Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': NOT FOUND. It could not be searched because this session's web-search budget was exhausted. It is not in my prior knowledge. Treat it as UNVERIFIED.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1. The effect is probably below the DC floor in 1-2 hours. A rough LPDDR5 I/O estimate:
- DC termination: about 0.4-0.5 pJ per driven '1' bit (0.5 V across roughly 80-90 ohm for one 156 ps unit interval at 6400 MT/s). With DBI-DC, random data leaves about 40% of lines high, all-zero leaves 0%.
- Toggle energy: about 0.25 pJ per bit for random data.
- Core-internal data dependence: plausibly the same order.
- Total random-minus-zero: about 0.003-0.006 J/GB at DC, which is about 8-15% of the 0.039 J/GB coefficient, not 40%.

At the measured 0.0072 J/GB half-width per hour, resolving 0.004 J/GB takes about 3+ hours per contrast. Splitting 1-2 hours across three fills makes each pairwise half-width worse than 0.0072 (about 0.009 J/GB). As designed, the most likely outcome is NOT RESOLVED. That would read as 'no data dependence' unless it is pre-declared as an upper bound.

2. Ghose's ordering does not carry over. DDR3L uses SSTL to VTT; LPDDR5 uses LVSTL to ground. If Apple's controller enables DBI-DC, all-ones goes out on the wire as all-zeros plus the DBI line, so I/O-wise all-ones is about all-zeros. The informative contrast is random vs zero, not ones vs zeros. Whether DBI or read-DBI is enabled on M3 Max is unknown.

3. Compute confound (Gregersen et al., up to about 40% GEMM power). The reduction kernel's ALUs and the SLC/fabric toggle differently on random vs zero data. That shifts the GPU rail and rest independently of DRAM. A DC or rest difference therefore cannot be called 'DRAM' without:
- subtracting the GPU-rail difference, which is precisely resolved at 1e-4 J/GB; and
- noting that rest still contains fabric, SLC and conversion loss.
The design needs a boundary-attribution rule written in advance.

4. Data-type hazards:
- All-ones in fp16, bf16 or fp32 is NaN, and random bits contain NaN/Inf at about 1/256 (bf16). NaN or denormal paths can change kernel timing.
- 85% of the GPU contrast was dispatch time, so any timing shift between fills dominates.
- Mitigation: stream an integer dtype with a bitwise reduction (OR or XOR) and require matched per-dispatch times as a validity check.

5. Residency and zero-page effects. Zero fills must be explicitly written (not zero-fill-on-demand or lazily allocated) and must stay resident across the 32 GiB ring. Under memory pressure macOS can compress idle pages. Run the existing geometry and residency check per fill.

6. The IOReport DRAM channel's existence and nature are unconfirmed. The repo's own fixtures handle a missing dram_w on this Mac. Whether it is a counter model or a sensed rail on M3 Max / macOS 26 is UNVERIFIED. The prediction 'IOReport will not show it' is an assumption, not established. Probe for the channel off-night first (software-only, no measurement Mac time needed beyond a 1-minute read).

7. The regime is latency-bound (about 110 GB/s). Higher-bandwidth streaming (larger dispatches near the SoC's peak) would put more GB through per hour. Because the noise is per unit time, that would shrink the J/GB half-width roughly in proportion. This is the single best lever.

Recommended re-scope:
- Two arms (random vs zero, integer dtype), ABBA order, bandwidth-bound geometry, pre-declared minimum detectable effect.
- Report the GPU rail (which will resolve the compute-side data dependence cleanly) and rest/DC as an upper bound.
- Drop all-ones, or keep it only as a DBI probe.

**Residual novelty**

Not refuted. Ghose et al. 2018 (DDR3L) settles that DRAM energy depends on data values and that standard DRAM power models ignore it. Gregersen et al. 2024 settles that GPU compute power depends on input data. What remains open is a measurement on Apple silicon with in-package LPDDR5 that splits data-value dependence across the GPU rail, rest and the DC input, and checks whether IOReport's DRAM channel (if one exists on M3 Max) tracks it.

The realistic value is different from what the claim says. The GPU-rail fill-pattern contrast is resolvable to about 1e-4 J/GB in minutes and is likely nonzero (Gregersen-type compute toggling). It also connects directly to Ed's interest in how surgical weight edits change power, because a weight edit changes the bit statistics of what is streamed. The DRAM/DC part is probably a 0.003-0.006 J/GB effect, below the 1-2 hour DC floor, so it will most likely yield an upper bound rather than a detection. It is still worth banking for instrument night 3, under these conditions:
- two arms (random vs zero, integer dtype)
- bandwidth-bound geometry
- the timing match pre-declared as a validity check
- an upper-bound reading rule
- the IOReport DRAM channel confirmed to exist before the night

---

## 18. Energy reveals routing: conditional-compute readout in MoE and Flash-Next (banked) (seat 2 (Opus))

### Fable 5.1 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- Ding, Xu, Shen, Ding, Fei, 'MoEcho: Exploiting Side-Channel Attacks to Compromise User Privacy in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs', arXiv 2508.15036 (CCS 2025). Four microarchitectural channels (CPU cache occupancy, Pageout+Reload; GPU perf counters r=0.993 expert-load correlation, TLB Evict+Reload 94.3% expert-sequence recovery) on DeepSeek-V2-Lite, Qwen1.5-MoE, TinyMixtral, DeepSeek-VL2; Threadripper Pro + A6000. Power/EM appear only as cited prior DNN side-channel work; no power, DVFS or storage/SSD channel is proposed or evaluated. Opened: abs page and HTML full text.
- Nuriyev & Kulp, 'Expert Selections In MoE Models Reveal (Almost) As Much As Text', arXiv 2602.04105 (Feb-Mar 2026). Token reconstruction from routing decisions alone (63.1% top-1 MLP; 91.2% top-1 / 94.8% top-10 sequence decoder, 32-token OpenWebText). Assumes expert selections are already leaked ('distributed inference and side channels'); no power or SSD channel studied. Opened.
- Kulp, MATS Summer 2026 stream page (matsprogram.org/stream/kulp-10): PROPOSES observing 'via radio, power fluctuations, acoustics, etc. which experts were used in each forward pass' then guessing tokens. Proposal only, no result. This is the closest public statement of the idea's privacy corollary; it is not a demonstration. Opened.
- Yona, Shumailov, Hayes, Carlini, 'Stealing User Prompts from Mixture of Experts', arXiv 2410.22884 (Oct 2024). Prompt extraction via torch.topk tie handling under Expert-Choice routing in a shared batch, two-layer Mixtral; O(VM^2) queries. Logical/batch channel, not physical; no power mention. Opened.
- Kyung, Yun, Ahn, 'SSD Offloading for LLM Mixture-of-Experts Weights Considered Harmful in Energy Efficiency', arXiv 2508.06978 (Aug 2025). Analytical/simulated (not measured): flash read 102.4 pJ/bit vs HBM3 4.2 pJ/bit; SSD-offloaded experts raise per-token decode energy up to ~12x vs HBM on a modeled DGX-H100 node; prefetch hides latency not energy; SSD idle power treated as negligible. Establishes that expert identity maps to energy ONLY when experts are not DRAM-resident. Opened: abs and HTML.
- Skliar et al., 'Mixture of Cache-Conditional Experts for Efficient Mobile Device Inference', arXiv 2412.00099 (TMLR 2025). Cache-aware routing on memory-constrained devices at batch 1; reports ~2x speedup, no energy or DRAM-traffic numbers. Opened abs only (full text not checked for energy).
- Alizadeh et al., 'LLM in a flash', arXiv 2312.11514 (ACL 2024). Flash-resident weights with windowing/row-column bundling; speed only (4-5x CPU, 20-25x GPU), no energy per token. Opened abs only.
- JouleWise internal bank (docs/research_question_bank.md): RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB (top-k 4/8/16 on Qwen3-30B-A3B as active-bytes dial, predicted ~+50 J per doubling), RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH (routing concentration under batching with a predeclared NULL at B=1 because one expert is far larger than the system-level cache so there is no reuse), RQ-D-A02 (function-preserving expert locality/dispatch order; rule: 'Claim dispatch-layout effects, never an expert's individual joules'), RQ-NEXT-BYTES-LAW (resident bytes enter only via effective-bandwidth drops from expert scatter or paging), C5-2.5d (mlx-dspark n-gram lookup path as contamination diagnostic). Block-6 draft pilot: Qwen3-30B-A3B reads 1.71 GB/token with 17.2 GB resident, effective 130-231 GB/s; powermetrics carries no DRAM key, so memory-chip energy is seen only at the wall/DC meter (meter minus rails).
- Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': NOT FOUND / UNVERIFIED. The session web-search budget was exhausted before this query executed and I have no independent knowledge of such a system or paper. Do not cite it as a premise in the research docs; mark it as an unverified pointer from Ed.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

1) First-order null for the headline claim on this hardware. Qwen3-30B-A3B (config: 48 layers, 128 experts, top-8, hidden 2048, moe_intermediate 768, norm_topk_prob true, no shared expert) reads 8 distinct experts per layer per token regardless of WHICH experts are chosen: 3*2048*768 = 4.72M params per expert, ~2.65 MB at 4.5 bits, 384 expert reads per token = ~1.02 GB of the measured 1.71 GB/token. All experts are resident in interleaved unified LPDDR5X at identical per-byte cost. A router-temperature edit at fixed k and fixed tokens therefore moves zero bytes and zero FLOPs; the predicted J/token delta is 0 to first order. The bank's own RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH already predicts this null at B=1 (expert 2.65 MB vs ~1.7 GB streamed per token, so no system-level-cache reuse between tokens). Second-order residuals (data-dependent ALU toggling on different weight bit patterns, DRAM bank/row conflicts from different addresses) are the classic DPA-scale signals: microwatts-to-milliwatts over microsecond windows, i.e. far below 0.1% of a 2-4 kJ episode and below the ~2 J repeat floor. 'Energy as a readout of routing identity' is thus expected to be unmeasurable; energy reads routing COUNT (k), which is trivially true and already banked as TOPK-KNOB.

2) Expert ablation is a disguised bytes knob. If an ablated expert is simply excluded from the candidate set, the router picks another and bytes are unchanged (null). If the ablated expert's compute is skipped when selected, bytes fall by that expert's load share: one expert at the mean 1/16 load across all layers removes ~6% of expert bytes = ~3.7% of 1.71 GB/token, above the bank's ~1.3% resolution at d=2048, but that is RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB at fractional k, not a routing readout. The idea's '>=1% at DC' estimate holds only for this bytes-removing variant.

3) 'DRAM and cache proxies' do not exist in the stack. powermetrics on Apple silicon exposes cpu/gpu/ane energy and no DRAM bandwidth or SLC counters (checked in block6 draft on rehearsal corpus18). The only memory-chip signal is meter-minus-rails, integrated per episode. There is no cache-hit proxy to correlate with.

4) Temporal resolution forbids the privacy corollary on this instrument class. powermetrics runs at 100 ms (joulewise/powermetrics_fiducial.py SAMPLING_INTERVAL_MS = 100); 30B-A3B decodes at roughly 75-135 tok/s (1.71 GB at 130-231 GB/s), so one sample spans 8-13 tokens and ~400-650 expert-layer decisions. Routing leakage via power would need per-layer (~0.2 ms) resolution and a channel whose first-order term is nonzero; neither holds. Testing the power-channel corollary needs an oscilloscope/shunt on the rails or an EM probe, which is a different project, not a JouleWise night.

5) Flash-Next paged-table arm: effect exists but is mostly not 'per lookup'. The lookup table (operator card: 8-bit 161.8 GB / 4-bit 136.2 GB, read one row at a time, B20) is SSD-paged. A single 4-16 KB random NVMe read costs on the order of 100-300 microjoules (drive active at ~1.5-3 W for ~50-100 us), versus ~100-400 mJ per decoded token for a ~1.5-7B-active core (bank planning levels 47 J/512 tok at 1.5B, 192 J at 7B), i.e. ~0.03-0.3% per lookup, near or below the ~0.05-0.1% repeat floor. The detectable term is instead the drive being held out of low-power state for the whole episode (~0.5-2 W, ~0.5-1.5% of a 3 kJ episode), plus, if the table lives on the external OWC/SN7100, the bus-powered enclosure's constant ~1-2 W, which shows at the DC meter as an offset unrelated to lookups. Confounders: APFS page cache in 128 GB unified memory will serve repeated rows from RAM on warm passes, so 'energy per lookup' must be designed cold-vs-warm with explicit purge, and iostat-based paging MB/s (already planned in MISSING-8 telemetry) is the right covariate. Also the lookup is a prefill-time/SSD-latency effect (B20 flags >15% of prefill time), so the cleaner metric is energy-vs-row-reads via time, not J/lookup. Flash-Next is not yet gold-parity validated, so this arm cannot be scheduled before R0-FN/R1-FN pass.

6) SSD-traffic as a privacy channel is primarily a software/timing channel (iostat, fs_usage, I/O latency), not an energy channel; MoEcho's Pageout+Reload is its close kin on CPU paging. Energy adds nothing an attacker could not get more easily from I/O counters.

7) Cost: with the identity arm predicted null and the k arm already banked, the 0.5-1 Mac-night buys at most a confirmatory null plus a Flash-Next drive-state measurement that the existing lookup telemetry (MISSING-8) will largely deliver as a by-product.

**Residual novelty**

No published work demonstrates a power or SSD-traffic channel for MoE routing (Kulp's MATS page proposes it; MoEcho and Nuriyev-Kulp assume or use other channels), so the literature does not refute the claim and refuted=false. But the claim as stated ('fixed-token energy depends on WHICH experts are used') is expected by physics to be null on a Mac with fully resident experts: identity is byte-symmetric; only count (k) and residency (paging) move energy, and both are already in the JouleWise bank (RQ-NEXT-TOPK-KNOB, RQ-NEXT-ROUTING×BATCH with its B=1 null, RQ-D-A02, RQ-NEXT-BYTES-LAW, C5-2.5d). What survives as genuinely new: (a) a cheap predeclared-null arm, 'router-temperature / router-weight edits at fixed k and fixed tokens produce no measurable J/token change at B=1', which is a useful falsification register for the interp-x-energy intersection Ed cares about (surgical router edits are energy-invisible unless they change k or residency); it costs one or two windows and could be appended to TOPK-KNOB rather than run as its own night; (b) a Flash-Next paged-table arm reframed from 'energy per lookup' to 'SSD active-state and page-cache energy as a function of row-read rate, cold vs warm', measured at the DC meter with iostat MB/s as covariate, after gold-parity validation; (c) the privacy corollary should be banked as out-of-scope for this instrument class (needs rail-shunt or EM probe at sub-ms resolution) with a note that an SSD-traffic channel is a software/timing channel, not an energy one. Recommend banking (a) and (b) as amendments to existing RQs and dropping the 'energy reveals routing identity' framing; record Ed's 'GPT-6 Astra' pointer as unverified.

### Opus 5.5 checker: not refuted

**Prior work**

- MoEcho (Ding et al., arXiv 2508.15036, abstract opened): shows MoE routing leaks prompts and responses through Cache Occupancy and Pageout+Reload on CPU, and through Performance Counter and TLB Evict+Reload on GPU, on DeepSeek-V2-Lite. The abstract names no power or energy channel. Pageout+Reload is a paging and residency channel, which weakens the claim that no storage-side channel exists. I read only the abstract, not the full paper.
- Nuriyev & Kulp, 'Expert Selections in MoE Models Reveal (Almost) as Much as Text' (arXiv 2602.04105v2, opened): with routing traces logged from gpt-oss-20b, a decoder recovers 91.2% of tokens top-1. Section 4 says power or EM side channels 'may' reveal expert selections but measures nothing on hardware. So the power channel is an open conjecture someone has already flagged, not a gap nobody has noticed.
- MATS stream by Gabriel Kulp (matsprogram.org/stream/kulp-10, opened): proposes, with no results, observing 'via radio, power fluctuations, acoustics' which experts each forward pass used, with an oscilloscope on the PSU and an EM probe at the die. An active group is pursuing the power-reads-routing idea with far better instruments than powermetrics.
- Gregersen, Patel, Choukse, 'Input-Dependent Power Usage in GPUs' (arXiv 2409.18324, opened): changing only the input values of a GEMM, at the same shape and size, moves GPU power by almost 40% through bit flips. Here it is a confound. Router and ablation edits change activation values even when bytes and FLOPs are fixed.
- LYNX (arXiv 2411.08982, opened): batch-aware expert selection. It notes that batching activates nearly all experts and remaps low-affinity assignments to cut the experts each batch invokes, for up to 1.30x throughput. That is prior art for 'routing edits change bytes per batched step'. The abstract mentions no energy.
- Alizadeh et al., 'LLM in a flash' (arXiv 2312.11514, opened): parameter paging from flash with windowing and row-column bundling. It reports speed only, with no energy figures in the abstract. It is prior art for flash-paged inference on limited-DRAM devices.
- Carlini et al. / 'Early Bird Catches the Leak' (arXiv 2409.20002): timing side channels on prompts through KV and semantic caches. I saw it only in search results and did not open it, so it is UNVERIFIED.
- TokenPowerBench (AAAI): an LLM inference power benchmark. The page was blocked, so it is UNVERIFIED, including whether it covers MoE routing.
- Ed's reference 'GPT-6 Astra can natively edit model weights': not found. The session's web-search budget ran out before I could search for it, and I will not guess its content.

**Physics or feasibility concerns**

FATAL FOR THE DESIGN AS WRITTEN. (1) Router temperature cannot change routing. In mlx-lm qwen3_moe (opened) the router computes gates = softmax(gate(x)), then inds = argpartition(...)[-k:], then renormalises when norm_topk_prob=True (true for Qwen3-30B-A3B per its HF config: 128 experts, k=8, 48 layers, hidden 2048, moe_intermediate 768). Softmax is monotonic, so scaling the logits by any temperature leaves the top-8 set unchanged for the same input. Only the mixing weights change, and through them the downstream activations. The routing change at that layer is exactly zero, and the bytes moved are unchanged. (2) At batch 1, energy is invariant to WHICH experts are used, to first order. Each token always streams exactly k=8 experts per layer, about 4.72M params each, roughly 2.6 MB at 4-bit, so about 1 GB of expert weights per token across 48 layers, from unified DRAM, and all 128 experts are resident because the model fits in RAM. An expert ablation just promotes the 9th-ranked expert, so it still moves 8 experts' bytes and FLOPs. Cross-token reuse cannot cut DRAM traffic either: about 1 GB streams between two visits to the same layer, which flushes the SLC (tens of MB). No routing-dependent shift of bytes from DRAM or SSD happens at batch 1, and the 'at least 1% at DC' expectation has no mechanism behind it. What remains is data-dependent switching (Gregersen et al.): different but statistically identical weight tensors plus changed activations. On a memory-bound decode I expect at most about 0.1%, near the replay floor (2 J on 2-4 kJ, i.e. 0.05-0.1%). Even if it were detected, it would read out activation statistics, not routing. (3) In the planned Flash-Next config the experts are resident too. The port doc's maxfit4 has 77.8 GB on Metal, and only the 320M x 160 bf16 n-gram table is paged. The n-gram rows are a hash of the last 3 token ids (16 rows per token), so at fixed tokens the table traffic is identical whatever the router or ablation edit. The table is a TOKEN readout, not a routing readout, so this half of the design cannot measure routing at all. (4) Energy per lookup cannot be resolved as SSD energy. An order-of-magnitude estimate, not measured: a random 4-16 KB NVMe read costs about 10 uJ, so about 0.2 mJ per token, against about 3-5 J per token for a 20 tok/s decode, a share of about 0.005%, 10-20x below the replay floor. The module's serial pread and per-token host sync (port doc: about 1-1.6 ms/token, about 3%) make DC energy per lookup mostly stall time multiplied by system static power, about 100x the SSD's own energy. A warm-versus-cold table contrast WILL be detectable at about 1-3% of DC energy, but what it measures is latency times idle power, already visible in timing (mlx-vlm #2045: 9.68 s of cold misses in a 4K prefill). (5) The rails do not include DRAM. The repo's powermetrics samplers are battery, cpu_power, gpu_power, ane_power and thermal. Apple silicon reports no DRAM or SSD rail, so 'DRAM and cache proxies' would come only from the DC-input difference or from bandwidth counters, which is weak attribution. (6) The privacy corollary is overstated. MoEcho already has a paging channel (Pageout+Reload). Powermetrics at about ms sampling, aggregated per rail, cannot plausibly tell expert identities apart at batch 1, when bytes and FLOPs per token are identical. Power-reads-routing needs oscilloscope or EM bandwidth, which is the Kulp group's plan. WHERE A REAL EFFECT EXISTS: any step that processes B>1 tokens at once (batched decode, speculative-verify of B drafts, small prefill chunks) reads the UNION of the selected experts. With uniform routing, k=8 of 128, about 51.6 experts per layer are touched at B=8. A concentration edit, such as a logit bias or masking to 16 experts, cuts this to about 16, so the step reads about 2 GB instead of about 6.6 GB of expert weights. DRAM energy is roughly 0.05-0.15 J/GB (assumed), so that is about 0.2-0.7 J per step plus time, far above the 0.0001 J/GB GPU-rail resolution and the replay floor. That union effect is mechanistically trivial (bytes cost energy), and LYNX covers it for throughput.

**Residual novelty**

Prior work does not refute the idea: nobody has measured energy as a readout of routing, and Nuriyev & Kulp plus the Kulp MATS stream only conjecture a power channel. The proposed design is broken on physics, though. Router temperature is a no-op on top-k membership. At batch 1 with resident experts, J/token is routing-invariant to first order. The Flash-Next n-gram traffic depends on the tokens, not on routing. Three things survive. (A) A cheap, clean, pre-registered NULL with privacy value. On Qwen3-30B-A3B at batch 1 and fixed tokens, expert ablations or logit-bias edits that change per-token expert identity (checked by logging the inds) should leave whole-machine and GPU-rail J/token unchanged within the 0.05-0.1% replay floor. That bounds the coarse-telemetry power side channel the MoE-leakage papers conjecture, and it reports any residual change as data-dependent switching. Cost: about 0.25 Mac-night. (B) The intersection Ed wants (surgical edits move power AND performance) exists only for multi-token steps. Sweep B in {1, 2, 4, 8} (batched decode or speculative verify) against router-concentration edits of graded strength, and measure DC plus GPU-rail energy per step against union-of-experts bytes and quality (KL or perplexity). That gives a quality-versus-energy frontier for routing edits on Apple unified memory. LYNX covers throughput, not energy. The effect is large and easy to detect, but mechanistically obvious. (C) For Flash-Next, report the energy of SSD-resident parameters as a stall-dominated DC quantity (warm table versus F_NOCACHE table, same tokens), clearly labelled as latency times static power, not SSD energy per lookup. Drop 'energy per lookup' and the routing framing for the table. Keep the whole idea banked, as Ed ranked it (3/4), and if run, do (A) first because it is the only part that directly answers the power-reads-routing question.
